Functorial resolution by torus actions Thanks: This research is supported by BSF grant 2014365
Jarosław Włodarczyk

Abstract
. We show a simple and fast embedded resolution of varieties and principalization of ideals in the language of torus actions on ambient, smooth varieties with SNC divisors. The canonical functorial resolution of varieties in characteristic zero is given by the introduced here operations of cobordant blow-ups with smooth weighted centers. The centers are defined by the geometric invariant measuring the singularities on smooth schemes with SNC divisors. As the result of the procedure, we obtain a smooth variety with a torus action and the exceptional divisor having simple normal crossings. Moreover, its geometric quotient is birational to the resolved variety, has abelian quotient singularities, and can be desingularized directly by combinatorial methods. The paper is based upon the ideas of the joint work with Abramovich and Temkin [ ATW19 ] and a similar result by McQuillan [ McQ19 ] on resolution in characteristic zero via stack-theoretic weighted blow-ups. As an application of the method, we show the resolution of certain classes of singularities in positive and mixed characteristic.



1.
Introduction

The significance of
G_{m}
-actions in birational geometry and their connection with Mori theory has already been identified by Reid, Thaddeus, and various other scholars (refer to
[
Tha94a
]
,
[
Tha94b
]
,
[
Tha96
]
,
[
Rei
]
,
[
DH98
]
). Particularly, Reid
[
Reid02
]
highlighted the role of weighted blowings up and flips in birational geometry utilizing
G_{m}
-actions. This notion was also reflected in the proof of the Weak Factorization theorem, which relied on the concept of
birational cobordism
and a pivotal role of
G_{m}
-action (
[
Wło00
]
,
[
Wło03
]
,
[
AKMW02
]
).

In the present paper, we propose approaching embedded resolution problems and the existing resolution algorithms from the point of view of torus actions. This is an alternative and, to a great extent, equivalent approach to the one pursued in a paper
[
McQ19
]
of McQuillan-Marzo, and a recent paper of Quek
[
Que20
]
, and most notably, in the series of papers by Abramovich-Temkin-Włodarczyk
[
ATW17
]
,
[
ATW20
]
,
[
ATW19
]
, where all kind of stack-theoretic blow-ups were considered to simplify resolution procedure or to adapt the algorithm to the relative situation of morphisms.
The introduced here definition of
cobordant blow-up
interprets the stack-theoretic weighted blow-ups, logarithmic Kummer blow-ups, and more general birational transformations of stacks
in terms of torus actions. Such interpretation leads to the presentation of the weighed blow-ups and other transformations as smooth birational cobordisms. The operation is done in the language of smooth schemes with torus actions in terms of simple transformation rules. In fact, only one chart is needed to describe the cobordant blow-up, which makes it a very simple tool for algorithmic computations
and possible theoretical implementations. The method can be applied to many existing and potential embedded resolution algorithms originally relying on smooth centers, regardless of characteristic.
It also avoids many problems associated with smooth centers and studied intensely in
[
Abh67
,
Moh87
,
Moh96
,
Hau98
,
Wło08
,
CP08
,
CP09
,
Cut11
,
BVU13
,
KM16
,
HP19
]
. Moreover, it is significantly faster and eliminates unnecessary blow-ups. Furthermore, the algorithm in characteristic zero has a very simple structure with the centers defined by a geometric invariant related to the weighted normal cone (see Section
4.1.1
).
The smooth centers are replaced with more general smooth weighted centers.
In fact, the present paper, similarly to
[
ATW19
]
, contains a highly simplified version of the strong Hironaka algorithm in characteristic zero. Recall that the standard Hironaka algorithm uses a rather complicated invariant where the geometry of singularities is mixed with the combinatorics of the monomials. In the present paper, similarly to
[
ATW19
]
and
[
McQ19
]
, the algorithm is purely geometric, with the combinatorial part hidden in the torus action or respectively in the stack-theoretic structure as, in the two papers above.

1.1.
Cobordant blow-ups

Unlike the formalism in
[
ATW19
]
, or in
[
McQ19
]
,
the resolution here is given in the language of schemes with torus actions and does not use stack-theoretic language, although, if needed, it can be converted into such. The algorithm is carried in the language of
cobordant blow-ups
of weighted centers on regular schemes. A similar operation in the language of stacks was independently studied in the paper of Quek-Rydh
[
QR22
]
.

The cobordant blow-ups of regular varieties have many advantages even when compared to the standard blow-ups of smooth blow-ups. They are described by particularly simple transformation rules with one chart only defined by the affine morphism. They carry important additional information about singularities which is contained in the
vertex
of the transformation.
The singularities along the vertex after the blow-up are to a great extent equivalent to the singularities at the center, and they are removed as typically the worst singularities in the resolution process.
Moreover the torus action contains additional additional important information for the resolution process.

All of this is especially beneficial for all kinds of generalizations of the resolution to classes of singularities in positive and mixed characteristic, foliations and others. In particular in Section
4
we show how to apply cobordant blow-ups with weighted centers to introduced here, class of
almost homogenous singularities
in any characteristic (see Theorems
4.2.2
,
4.2.3
).
This class includes, in particular, the isolated singularities whose generalized weighted tangent cone also has an isolated singularity. Moreover, the approach can be generalized to the case where the equimultiple locus of the subscheme and its weighted tangent cone coincide (see Lemma
4.3.1
).

The language and the formalism of the cobordant blow-ups are related to the notion of
birational cobordism
introduced as the main tool in the proof of the Weak factorization theorem (
[
Wło00
]
,
[
Wło03
]
,
[
AKMW02
]
). On the other hand when looking from a more general perspective of Cox rings of morphisms in the subsequent paper
[
Wło23
]
one shows that any proper birational morphism of normal noetherian schemes has such a cobordant presentation called
cobordization
, where the considered here cobordant blow-up of weighted center is the cobordization of the standard weighted blow-up. Moreover the theorems on the resolution from Chapter
4
are extended in
[
Wło23
]
to generalized cobordant blow-ups of locally monomial ideals.

1.2.
Rees algebras

Another novel of the paper is the use of rational Rees algebras, giving a fast algorithm with automatic uniqueness. One shall mention that the rational Rees algebras was also briefly used in the first version of
[
ATW19
]
to interpret the algebra of the center.

Recall that there are presently two approaches to the uniqueness and glueing of the embedded resolutions. The first method is known as ”Hironaka’s trick” which uses an equivalence relation defined by permissible blow-ups. It was used in the original Hironaka’s approach, and in the subsequent papers of Bierstone-Milman and Villamayor and others (see
[
Hir64
]
,
[
Vil89
]
,
[
BM97
]
,
[
EH02
]
,
[
EV03
]
). The other method is known as ”homogenization-tuning” trick was introduced by the author in
[
Wło05
]
, and then used in the papers of Kollar
[
Kol07
]
, Bierstone-Milman
[
BM08
]
, and others. This approach is also used in
[
ATW17
]
and
[
ATW19
]
.
It relies on the existence of local automorphisms of the homogenized ideals and functoriality for étale morphisms.
Both approaches hinge on the induction on the dimension of ambient variety. The inductively built centers living on different hypersurfaces of maximal contact are compared via equivalence relations or étale maps.

With our approach the centers are unique despite being constructed using noncanonical itermediate steps. Moreover what is important no comparison between the intermediate steps is needed. Instead all the operations are related directly to the constructed canonical resolution center.

Recall that the integrally graded Rees algebras have been used for some time in the resolution algorithms(
[
EV07
]
,
[
Vil08
]
,
[
BV11
]
,
[
CP09
]
,
[
KM16
]
). They were also used recently in the approach by Quek for the weighted logarithmic resolution
[
Que20
]
. They are also considered in the paper by Quek and Rydh
[
QR22
]
. There are however several advantages of the Rees algebras with rational gradations. The very first one is that the resolution center and the resolution invariant can be conveniently defined in this language as the natural generalization of the order.

1.2.1.
Definition of the invariant

Let us start with the definition of the order of an ideal
{\mathcal{I}}
at a point
p\in X
:

{\operatorname{ord}}_{p}({\mathcal{I}})=\max\{a\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}\mid{\mathcal{I}}\subset m_{p}^{a}\},

where
m_{p}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X,p}
is the maximal ideal of the point
p
in the local ring
{\mathcal{O}}_{X,p}
.
This can be written as

{\operatorname{ord}}_{p}({\mathcal{I}})=\max\{a\in{\mathbb{Q}}_{>0}\mid{\mathcal{I}}t^{a}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[m_{p}t]\},

where
R={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[m_{p}t]
is the Rees algebra generated by the first gradation
m_{p}t
, where
t
is the dummy variable. Equivalently one writes the latter as

{\operatorname{ord}}_{p}({\mathcal{I}})=\max\{a\in{\mathbb{Q}}_{>0}\mid{\mathcal{I}}t\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[m_{p}t^{1/a}]\},

If the divisors are not present that the resolution invariant of
{\mathcal{I}}
at
p
can be written simply as

(1)

\displaystyle{\operatorname{inv}}_{p}({\mathcal{I}}):=\max\{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})\mid{\mathcal{I}}t\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[x_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{{\operatorname{int}}}\},

where
a_{1}\leq\ldots\leq a_{k}
are rational numbers ordered lexicographically and the maximum is considered over all the partial coordinate systems
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
. Here ”
{\operatorname{int}}
” stands for the corresponding integral closure. The present definition is equivalent to the one from
[
ATW19
]
, which was considered in the different language. Note that the powers of
t
indicate the weights of the elements. In particular the weight of
x_{i}
is
1/a_{i}
with the weight
1
assigned to
{\mathcal{I}}
.

1.2.2.
Rational Rees algebras

One of the benefits of Rees algebras is that their generators lie in the different rational gradations which often leads to a simpler presentation. Additional advantage of the rational Rees algebras is that no rescaling is needed and we can always get the simplest possible set of generators by extending the gradation. This makes the computations of the centers very straightforward.

This could be illustrated by the Veronese embedding. When passing from the graded algebra
R=k[x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}]=\bigoplus_{d\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}}R_{d}
to the subgradation
R^{(n)}=\bigoplus_{d\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}}R_{nd},
as in the
n
-tuple Veronese embedding we obtain the graded algebra
R^{(n)}
with a very cumbersome set of generators given by all monomials of degree
n
and describing the same
\operatorname{Proj}(R^{(n)})=\operatorname{Proj}(R)
. Now when we start from
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded algebra
R^{(n)}
written as
R^{(n)}=\bigoplus_{d\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}}R_{nd}t^{d}
for the dummy variable
t
then extending the gradation and passing to the integral closure
R^{(n)}[t^{1/n}]^{{\operatorname{int}}}
one obtains back the algebra
R=k[x_{1}t^{1/n},\ldots,x_{k}t^{1/k}]
in the graded form with respect to
t^{1/k}
and with the nice set of generators, and giving a nicer presentation of the same object
\operatorname{Proj}(R)
.

The process avoids various multiple rescalings which often leads to the
increased computational complexity.
Since no rescaling is needed the construction of the center can be obtained in a minimalistic way by a series of transformations called
milling
.

1.2.3.
Milling

The concept behind
milling
is to systematically decompose the elements of the ideal
\mathcal{I}
, as represented by the Rees algebra
R=\mathcal{O}_{X}[\mathcal{I}t]
, step by step, into elements of smaller gradations. This process is done using graded differential operators and is carried out with the objective of obtaining a smooth weighted center. A similar idea was considered in the first version of
[
ATW19
]
to illustrate a different construction method. Note that in our process only the final stage is canonical, and all the intermediate steps rely on the choices made for the convenient minimalistic computations.

Consider the admissibility inclusion

(2)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}t\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[x_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{{\operatorname{int}}}

as in the above definition (1) of the resolution invariant
{\operatorname{inv}}_{p}({\mathcal{I}})
.
The Rees algebra of the form

{\mathcal{A}}:={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[x_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{{\operatorname{int}}}

is called the
Rees center
. If the inclusion (2) holds then we say that the center
{\mathcal{A}}
is
admissible
for
{\mathcal{I}}
at
p
.
If additionally
{\operatorname{inv}}_{p}({\mathcal{I}})=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})
as in (1) then the Rees center
{\mathcal{A}}
on the right will be called a
maximal admissible center
. This terminology is to a great extent equivalent to the one introduced in
[
ATW19
]
in the language of
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideals.

The algorithm uses
the basic concepts of the standard Hironaka algorithm like the admissibility, coefficient ideal, maximal contact, from
[
Hir64
]
,
[
Vil89
]
,
[
BM97
]
,
[
Wło05
]
,
[
EH02
]
,
[
EV03
]
,
[
Kol07
]
, which are redefined from the perspective of rational Rees algebras and cobordant blow-ups.

1.2.4.
The graded differential operators

The idea of
milling
is to construct the maximal admissible center through a step-by-step process that involves enlarging the initial Rees algebra
R_{1}:={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[{\mathcal{I}}t]\subset{\mathcal{A}}
, while keeping
{\mathcal{A}}
unchanged. To achieve this, we perform actions on both sides of the admissibility inclusion using graded differential operators. These operators preserve the right side
{\mathcal{A}}
of the inclusion while expanding the left side. They eventually lead to a situation where both sides become equal. As a result, the maximal admissible center is attained through a recursive process. Importantly, this process is dependent solely on
{\mathcal{I}}
, establishing the uniqueness of the maximal admissible center and the canonical invariant.

To illustrate the action of graded operators consider the inclusion

(3)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}t^{a}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[m_{p}t],

where
a={\operatorname{ord}}_{p}({\mathcal{I}})
. Here we can act on both sides of the inclusion by the differential operators
t^{-1}D_{X}
for
{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
being the sheaf of the differential operators on smooth variety
X
over a field
k
, and
a\in{\mathbb{N}}
. The right side is preserved by this action as
t^{-1}D_{X}(m_{p}t)^{a}\subset(m_{p}t)^{a-1}
while we obtain new elements on the left side.
In particular, we derive the inclusion of
t
-gradations
{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{a-1}({\mathcal{I}})t\subset m_{p}t
.

When rescaling (3) we obtain the inclusion

{\mathcal{I}}t\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[m_{p}t^{1/a}],

with the action of the differential operators
t^{-1/a}D_{X}
to get

{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{a-1}({\mathcal{I}})t^{1/a}\subset m_{p}t^{1/a}.

Similarly if
{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[x_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{{\operatorname{int}}}
is maximal admissible for
{\mathcal{I}}
then
a_{1}
is necessarily the order of
{\mathcal{I}}
at
p
. We can act by the operators
t^{-1/a}D_{X}
on both sides of the admissibility relation

{\mathcal{I}}t\subset{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[x_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{{\operatorname{int}}}

and obtain the inclusion of
t^{1/a_{1}}
gradations

(4)

\displaystyle D_{X}^{a_{1}-1}({\mathcal{I}})t^{1/a_{1}}\subset{\mathcal{A}}_{1/a_{1}}t^{1/a_{1}}

where
{\mathcal{A}}=\bigoplus_{a}{\mathcal{A}}_{a}t^{a}
.

1.2.5.
Maximal contacts

The
maximal contacts
of
{\mathcal{I}}
at
p
in our approach should be thought of as simply the graded coordinates
x_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}
in a certain presentation of a maximal admissible center
{\mathcal{A}}\subset{\mathcal{I}}t
at
p
. Unlike the usual definition this approach makes sense in a nonzero characteristic.
The maximal contacts are defined in terms of the recursively constructed algebras on the left side of the admissibility inclusion. In particular, in view of (1) the first maximal contact is defined by a coordinate

x_{1}\in T^{1/a_{1}}(R_{1}):=D_{X}^{a_{1}-1}({\mathcal{I}})\subset{\mathcal{A}}_{1/a_{1}}

in gradation
t^{1/a_{1}}
.

Observe that the notions of the
admissibility
, the
order
, the
invariant
and the
maximal contact
can be considered in the general context of Rees algebra
R
replacing
{\mathcal{I}}t
with
R
in the relevant definitions.

Consequently, the notion of
i
-th maximal contact
x_{i}
for any Rees algebra shall be understood as the coordinate in
t^{1/a_{i}}
-gradation of its maximal admissible center
{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[x_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{{\operatorname{int}}}\supset{\mathcal{I}}t
such that
x_{i}
is independent of the previous maximal contacts
x_{1},\ldots,x_{i-1}
.

1.2.6.
Coefficient ideals

The
coefficient ideal
of an algebra
R
is a way to produce larger Rees algebras containing the relevant maximal contacts.

Given any Rees algebra
R=\bigoplus R_{a}t^{a}
, and any graded coordinate
{x}t^{1/a}
the coefficient ideal
C_{xt^{1/a_{1}}}(R)
of
R
with respect to
xt^{1/a}
is generated by
xt^{1/a}
and all the derivations obtained by the action of the graded derivations
D_{xt^{1/a}}:=t^{-1/a_{1}}\partial_{x}
. Consequently, by the above if
R\subset{\mathcal{A}}
and
x_{1}t^{1/a_{1}}
is the first maximal contact of
R

then
C_{x_{1}t^{1/a_{1}}}(R)\subset{\mathcal{A}}
.

The computation of the coefficient ideal is even simpler when we pass to the completion of the local ring with respect to
(x)
or maximal ideal
m_{p}
so that the splitting gives the inclusion
\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,p}/(x)\subset\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,p}
. In such a case
\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,p}\cdot C_{xt^{1/a_{1}}}(R)
is generated by the maximal contact and the coefficients in the graded form of the generators of
R
with respect to the powers
(xt^{1/a})^{i}
. These coefficients by splitting live on
\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,p}\cdot R_{|V(x)}\subset\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,p}\cdot R
. (See Lemma
3.5.7
, Examples
3.2.2
,
3.2.4
,
3.2.6
)

1.3.
Construction of the center

Going back to the milling process we put

\displaystyle R_{1}:={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[{\mathcal{I}}t]\subset{\mathcal{A}},

\displaystyle R_{2}:=C_{x_{1}t^{1/a_{1}}}(R_{1})\subset{\mathcal{A}}=C_{x_{1}t^{1/a_{1}}}({\mathcal{A}})

\displaystyle\ldots

\displaystyle R_{i+1}:=C_{x_{i}t^{i/a_{i}}}(R_{i})\subset{\mathcal{A}}=C_{x_{i}t^{1/a_{i}}}({\mathcal{A}})

\displaystyle\ldots

\displaystyle R_{k+1}={\mathcal{A}}.

The process will continue until
R_{k+1|V(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})}=0
and thus
R_{k+1}={\mathcal{A}}

which completes the procedure. In this recursion,
a_{i}={\operatorname{ord}}_{p}(R_{i|V(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i-1})})
and the
i
-the maximal contact
x_{i}t^{1/a_{i}}
is defined so that the restriction
x_{i|V(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i-1})}
is a local parameter in the
t^{1/a_{i}}
-gradation of the algebra obtained by the action of
t^{-1/a_{i}}D_{x_{i}},\ldots,t^{-1/a_{i}}D_{x_{n}}
on

R_{i|V(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i-1})}
. In the process we change the coordinate system in such a way that gradually step by step all the maximal contacts are included. These changes however do not affect
{\mathcal{A}}
just its representation. (See Examples
3.2.2
,
3.2.4
,
3.2.6
).
Again the above process in a non-SNC setting shall be compared to the construction of the algebra associated with the center in the first version of
[
ATW19
]
.

The whole algorithm is independent of the a priori chosen maximal admissible center
{\mathcal{A}}
. At the same time it is done inside of
{\mathcal{A}}
which is then uniquely determined as the terminal algebra of the recursive process.

1.4.
Rees centers and cobordant blow-up

The computed maximal admissible Rees center
{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[x_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{{\operatorname{int}}}
determines the extended Rees algebra

{\mathcal{A}}^{\operatorname{ext}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{-1/w_{A}},x_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}],

where
w_{A}={\operatorname{lcm}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})
is the least common multiple of the rational numers
a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}
.

Now the rescaled integral extended Rees algebra algebra

{\mathcal{O}}_{B}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{-1},x_{1}t^{w_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{w_{k}}],

with
w_{i}=w_{A}/a_{i}
defines the desired transformation of the
full cobordant blow-up

B=\operatorname{Spec}_{X}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{-1},x_{1}t^{w_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{w_{k}}])\to X.

Here the new coordinates are
t^{-1},x_{1}t^{w_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{w_{k}}
, where
t^{-1}
describes the exceptional divisor
D:=V_{B}(t^{-1})
, and the
vertex of the cobordant blow-up
is defined
as
V:=V_{B}(x_{1}t^{w_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{w_{k}})
.

1.5.
Controlled transforms

Rescaling the admissibility inclusion
{\mathcal{I}}t\subset{\mathcal{A}}^{{\operatorname{ext}}}
leads to
{\mathcal{I}}t^{w_{A}}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
. Thus the full transform
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}\cdot{\mathcal{I}}
of
{\mathcal{I}}
is divisible by the power
(t^{-1})^{w_{A}}
of the exceptional divisor
D=V_{B}(t^{-1})
. The result of this factorization
\sigma^{c}({\mathcal{I}}):={\mathcal{O}}_{B}{\mathcal{I}}t^{w_{A}}
is called the
controlled transform
of
{\mathcal{I}}
.
Thus the admissibility condition turns into the condition for the controlled transform.

Also the new coordinates
x_{1}^{\prime}:=x_{1}t^{w_{1}},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{k}:=x_{k}t^{w_{k}}
are simply the
controlled transforms
of
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
.
Consequently the controlled
transform
\sigma^{c}({\mathcal{A}})
of the center
{\mathcal{A}}
can be written at the vertex
V
as
\sigma^{c}({\mathcal{A}})={\mathcal{O}}_{B}[x^{\prime}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]
.
Similarly the derivations
D_{x_{i}^{\prime}}=t^{-w_{i}}D_{x_{i}}=\sigma^{c}(D_{x_{i}})
on
B
are the controlled transforms of
D_{x_{i}}
.

As a result the coefficient ideals
C_{x_{i}t^{1/a_{i}}}(R_{i})
commute with the operation of the controlled transform:
\sigma^{c}(C_{x_{i}t^{1/a_{i}}}(R_{i}))=C_{x^{\prime}_{i}t^{1/a_{i}}}(\sigma^{c}(R_{i})),
and the entire milling algorithm commutes at the points of the vertex
V
converging from
\sigma^{c}(R_{1})={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\sigma^{c}({\mathcal{I}})t]
to the maximal admissible center
\sigma^{c}(R_{k+1})=\sigma^{c}({\mathcal{A}})
and giving the same value of the invariant

{\operatorname{inv}}(\sigma^{c}(R))=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})={\operatorname{inv}}(R)

for
\sigma^{c}(R)
at the vertex
V
as for
{\mathcal{I}}
at the center
V({\mathcal{A}})
.

1.6.
Resolution principle for the resolution in characteristic zero

The resolution algorithm in characteristic zero can be stated as follows:

For any ideal
{\mathcal{I}}
and a point
p
consider a unique maximal admissible center
{\mathcal{A}}
. Then

(1)

The vanishing locus
V({\mathcal{A}})
describes the locus of the points
q
where
{\operatorname{inv}}_{q}({\mathcal{I}})={\operatorname{inv}}_{p}({\mathcal{I}})
is constant and attains its maximal value in a certain neighborhood
U
of
p
. Thus
{\operatorname{inv}}_{p}({\mathcal{I}})
is upper semicontinuous and attains finitely many values along the maximal admissible centers.

(2)

The full cobordant of
B
at
{\mathcal{A}}
corresponds to the rescaled algebra
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
of
{\mathcal{A}}^{\operatorname{ext}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}
.

(3)

Consider the Rees center
{\mathcal{A}}
associated with the maximum
{\operatorname{maxinv}}_{X}({\mathcal{I}})
of the invariant
{\operatorname{inv}}
on
X
. After the full cobordant blow-up
B
of
X
at
{\mathcal{A}}
the invariant
{\operatorname{inv}}
of the controlled transform
\sigma^{c}({\mathcal{I}}):={\mathcal{O}}_{B}\cdot{\mathcal{I}}t^{w_{A}}
attains its maximum at the vertex
V
from
B
.
This maximum is equal to to

{\operatorname{maxinv}}_{B}(\sigma^{c}({\mathcal{I}}))={\operatorname{maxinv}}_{X}({\mathcal{I}})={\operatorname{inv}}_{p^{\prime}}(\sigma^{c}({\mathcal{I}}))

for any
p^{\prime}\in V
.

(4)

The invariant
``{\operatorname{inv}}"
drops for the
cobordant blow-up

B_{+}:=B\smallsetminus V
after removing the vertex
V
, that is

{\operatorname{maxinv}}_{B}(\sigma^{c}({\mathcal{I}}))<{\operatorname{maxinv}}_{X}({\mathcal{I}})

leading to the resolution of singularities.

(5)

The maximal admissible center for the ideal
\sigma^{c}({\mathcal{I}})
at
V
is given by the controlled transform
\sigma^{c}(A)
of the center
{\mathcal{A}}
so we have maximal admissibility inclusion
\sigma^{c}({\mathcal{I}})\subset\sigma^{c}({\mathcal{A}})
.

1.7.
Resolution principle in general

When considering maximal admissible centers in characteristic zero for an ideal
{\mathcal{I}}
we see a strong correlation between the singularities at the vertex and the center. The singularities along the maximal admissible center
V({\mathcal{A}})
are the same as the singularities at the vertex
V
in the sense they have the same invariant.

This principle is a part of a more general picture and can be pursued in a nonzero characteristic which leads to various types of resolution and reduction theorems. It relies on the following observations:

(1)

Let
D=V_{B}(t^{-1})
be the exceptional divisor. The vertex
V
consists of two parts:

V\smallsetminus D\subset B_{-}:=B\smallsetminus D=X\times{\mathbb{A}}^{1}

is isomorphic to
V\smallsetminus D\simeq V({\mathcal{A}})\times{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
, showing that the singularities of
V({\mathcal{I}})
at
V({\mathcal{A}})
are equivalent up to smooth projection to the singularities of
the strict transform

V(\sigma^{s}({\mathcal{I}}))
at
V\smallsetminus D
. Here the strict transform
\sigma^{s}({\mathcal{I}})
is obtained by the factoring out the maximal powers of the exceptional divisors from the functions in the full transform
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}\cdot{\mathcal{I}}
.

(2)

The exceptional divisor
D=V_{B}(t^{-1})
is isomorphic to the normal weighted bundle
N_{X/V({\mathcal{A}})}
of
X
at
V({\mathcal{A}})
. (Lemma
4.1.5
)
The
singularities of the intersection

V(\sigma^{s}({\mathcal{I}}))\cap D
at
V_{D}:=V\cap D\subset D
are isomorphic to the singularities of the normal weighted cone
C_{V({\mathcal{A}})}(V({\mathcal{I}}))\subset N_{X/V({\mathcal{A}})}
of the
V({\mathcal{I}})
at
V({\mathcal{A}})
. (Lemma
4.1.7
)

This allows to resolve or improve singularities if they are controlled along the center and when the singularities of its normal weighted cone are controlled at the center as well. The method works for some classes of singularities. In general, in a nonzero characteristic we may have some automorphisms of the normal cone which prevent an apparent improvement of singularities.

1.8.
SNC divisors

Another novelty of this paper is the direct treatment of the exceptional SNC divisors without any additional structures. In
[
ATW19
]
, or in
[
McQ19
]
, this problem is omitted and the simple resolution is obtained without any conditions on the exceptional divisors.
In the classical Hironaka approach the algorithm requires the exceptional divisor must have SNC crossings, and the process is controlled by a certain inductively defined filtration on the set of the exceptionals which is arguably the most cumbersome part of the proof. In this paper no structure is needed and a geometric invariant is a simple function of the subvariety or the ideal and the set of the exceptional divisors.
Consequently, the embedded and nonembedded resolution of varieties and principalization of ideals is obtained in the stronger form where the centers have SNC with the strict transforms, and the inverse image of the ideal is given by an effective SNC divisor.

The definition of the invariant
{\operatorname{inv}}_{p}({\mathcal{I}})
and the maximal admissible centers are adapted to the regular schemes with SNC divisor.
In such a case we only consider the coordinate systems where each divisorial component at a given point is represented by one of the coordinates. We shall also think of the divisorial coordiantes as infinitesimally heavier than the other ones.

Consequently in the admissibility relation

{\mathcal{I}}t\subset{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[x_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{{\operatorname{int}}}

we associate with the any free, that is non-divisorial coordinate
x_{i}
the element
b_{i}=a_{i}
and with divisorial coordinate
x_{j}
the slightly ”heavier” symbol
b_{j}=a_{j+}
.
We assume here that
a_{+}>a
for any
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
and if
b>a
for
a,b\in{\mathbb{R}}
then
b>a_{+}

This gives a
vector
(b_{1},\ldots,b_{k})
and we assume that
b_{1}\leq\ldots\leq b_{k}
.
The invariant in such a case can be written as

{\operatorname{inv}}_{p}({\mathcal{I}})=\max\{(b_{1},\ldots,b_{k})\mid{\mathcal{I}}t\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[x_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{{\operatorname{int}}}\}.

The main point of this construction is that we obtain still upper semicontinuous invariant, which drops after a cobordant blow-up at the associated maximal admissible center. Moreover, the whole algorithm is only marginally affected by this modification of the invariant. The only essential change lies in the construction of maximal contact allowing additional divisorial coordinates tangential to
T^{1/a_{i}}(R_{i})
.

1.9.
Comparing to existing strategies and ideas

The ideas of this paper stem mostly from
[
ATW19
]
and the joint project with Abramovich and Temkin.
The main difference here is the use of cobordant blow-ups, rational Rees algebras for the construction and uniqueness of the centers, and the use of the SNC divisors giving stronger results on resolution and principalization without changes in the complexity of the algorithm. Moreover, the paper extends some ideas to a more general context of schemes in any characteristic. In the case where the exceptional divisors are ignored we obtain the same centers as in
[
ATW19
]
.

The cobordant blow-ups were considered independently by Quek and Rydh in
[
QR22
]
from a slightly different angle. They provide a convenient presentation of the stack theoretic weighted blow-ups. Again the ideas were born from the method in
[
ATW19
]
.
One shall stress that using stack-theoretic quotients
does not give here any particular benefit. Instead, some of the information is lost in the process. It is mostly used in the historic context of the established notion.

In
[
ATW19
]
the construction of the center was achieved by a different inductive argument in a different language and its uniqueness was proven by using the idea of
homogenization ideals
from
[
Wło05
]
. The centers were represented by the
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideals
{\mathcal{J}}=(x_{1}^{1/w_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}^{1/w_{k}})
.
In the very first version of
[
ATW19
]
this construction was related to the rescaled algebra of the center
{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathcal{J}}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[x_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{{\operatorname{int}}}
for an ideal
{\mathcal{I}}
obtained by applying the saturated weighted derivatives
{\mathcal{D}}_{x_{i}t^{1/a_{1}}}
to
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[{\mathcal{I}}t]
, and the center
{\mathcal{J}}=(x_{1}^{1/w_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}^{1/w_{k}})
in the
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideal form with
w_{1}a_{1}=\ldots=w_{k}a_{k}
. Such an interpretation is valid when there are no SNC divisors. This was done as an illustration of our method, was not used in the context of the resolution strategy and thus was omitted in the next versions of
[
ATW19
]
.

The method in this paper is designed to derive directly in an efficient way the invariant and the unique maximal center
{\mathcal{A}}
from
{\mathcal{I}}
using the minimalistic transformations determined solely by
{\mathcal{I}}
without any references to prior constructions. The transformations are done in SNC setting and applied to generators only. As a consequence, all the intermediate steps are easy to compute but non-canonical and only the final center is automatically unique, since the process is independent of the maximal admissible center containing all the transitional algebras (see Examples
3.2.2
,
3.2.4
,
3.2.6
).

The compact formula for the coefficient ideal which is generated by the coefficients and maximal contact can be linked to the Bierstone-Milman presentation with the important distinction that it involves no factorization of monomials and the coefficient presentation is done on the completion of the local ring of the original variety in the language of Rees algebras. The Bierstone-Milman coefficient ideal lives on a hypersurface of maximal contact and is generated solely by the adjusted coefficients (
[
BM91
]
,
[
BM97
]
).

This type of Bierstone-Milman approach to the coefficient ideal is much more efficient for computations and practical implementations. Recall that most of the resolution strategies rely on the Villamayor approach to the coefficient ideal with rescaling of the ideals (see
[
Vil89
]
,
[
Wło05
]
,
[
EH02
]
,
[
EV03
]
,
[
Kol07
]
,
[
BM08
]
. This strategy was also used in
[
ATW19
]
. Each coefficient ideal typically increases the grading from
n
to
n!
which severely restricts the implementability of the algorithm. This is due to the simple fact that already
(5!)!
exceeds
10^{97}
which is according to the Standard Model of particles the assumed maximal estimated number of the subatomic particles in the universe.
In practice, computing even the first coefficient ideal could be tedious for the relatively small gradings say
n\geq 7
.
Here the grading
n
is typically given by the order of the ideal
{\mathcal{I}}
at a point and is estimated by the degrees of its generators.

In their paper
[
QR22
]
, Rydh and Quek observed that the canonical projection
B\to{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
determines the
deformation to the normal weighted cone
. (see Remark
4.1.11
).
They also noticed that weighted blow-up can be interpreted in terms of the toric combinatorial construction of Cox rings.

The approach was pursued in the joint work
[
AQ21
]
of Abramovich-Quek based on the Satriano combinatorial method from
[
Sat13
]
. Finally in the subsequent paper
[
Wło23
]
, we give a general formula for the realization of any proper birational morphism by the torus action using the Cox rings.
In the paper
[
Wło23
]
we also extend the methods and results of the
weighted normal cone
from Section
4
(Theorems
4.2.2
,
4.2.3
,
4.3.1
) to the context of the blow-ups of any locally monomial ideals.

1.10.
The output of the algorithm

The resolution algorithm outputs a smooth scheme with a torus action and admitting a geometric quotient having abelian quotient singularities and birational to the original scheme. The quotient can be directly resolved by canonical combinatorial methods in any characteristic as in
[
Wło20
, Theorem 7.17.1]
, or in
[
BR19
]
by the destackification method of Bergh and Rydh.

1.10.1.
Aknowledgements

The author would like to thank Dan Abramovich, Kenji Matsuki, Mircea Mustaţǎ, Michael Temkin, and Willem Veys and many others for helpful discussions and suggestions.

1.11.
Main results in characteristic zero

The following theorems extend the results and the resolution method by stack-theoretic weighted blow-ups from

[
ATW19
, Section 1.2]
in the language of torus actions and SNC divisors.

1.11.1.
Functorial principalization

Theorem 1.11.2
.

Let
X
be a smooth variety over a field
k
of characteristic zero, and
E
be a simple normal crossing (SNC) divisor and
{\mathcal{I}}
be an ideal sheaf on
X
. There exists a canonical principalization of
{\mathcal{I}}
, that is a sequence of cobordant blow-ups (Definition
2.4.7
) at smooth weighted centers

X=X_{0}\leftarrow X_{1}\leftarrow\ldots\leftarrow X_{k}=X^{\prime},\quad\hfill{(*)}

such that:

(1)

The torus
T_{i}=G_{m}^{i}
acts on
X_{i}
, with finite stabilizers, where
T_{0}=1
,
T_{i+1}=T_{i}\times G_{m}
, ans
T:=T_{k}
so that the geometric quotient (space of orbits)
X_{i}/T_{i}
exists.

(2)

Set
{\mathcal{I}}_{0}:={\mathcal{I}}
. For
i\geq 1
, the ideals
{\mathcal{I}}_{i}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{i}}
, defined as
{\mathcal{I}}_{i}:={\mathcal{O}}_{X_{i}}\cdot{\mathcal{I}}_{i-1}
are
T_{i}
-stable.

(3)

Set
E_{0}:=E
, and let
E_{i}
, for
i\geq 1
, be the total transform of
E_{i-1}
. The divisors
E_{i}
on
X_{i}
are
T_{i}
-stable and have SNC.

(4)

The smooth weighted centers
{\mathcal{J}}_{i}
of the cobordant blow-ups
X_{i}\leftarrow X_{i+1}
are compatible with
E_{i}
(Definition
3.1.2
), and are
T_{i}
-stable. Moreover
V({\mathcal{J}}_{i})\subseteq V({\mathcal{I}}_{i})
.

(5)

{\mathcal{O}}_{X^{\prime}}\cdot{\mathcal{I}}={\mathcal{I}}_{D^{\prime}}:={\mathcal{O}}_{X^{\prime}}(-D^{\prime})
is the ideal of an SNC divisor
D^{\prime}
whose components are the strict transforms of the components of the total transform
E^{\prime}
of
E
. Moreover there is a
T
-equivariant isomorphism over
X\smallsetminus V({\mathcal{I}})
:

X^{\prime}\smallsetminus D^{\prime}\simeq(X\smallsetminus V({\mathcal{I}}))\times T.

(6)

The sequence (*) defines the sequence of weighted blow-ups on the induced geometric quotients

X=X/T_{0}\leftarrow X_{1}/T_{1}\leftarrow\ldots\leftarrow X_{k}/T_{k}=X^{\prime}/T,
such that
{\mathcal{O}}_{X^{\prime}/T^{\prime}}\cdot{\mathcal{I}}={\mathcal{I}}_{D^{\prime\prime}}
is the ideal of a locally principal divisor
D^{\prime\prime}:=D^{\prime}/T\subset X^{\prime}/T^{\prime}
, where
D^{\prime}
is a
T
-stable SNC divisor on
X^{\prime}
, and
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\cdot{\mathcal{I}}_{D^{\prime\prime}}={\mathcal{I}}_{D^{\prime}}
.

(7)

The sequence (*) defines the sequence of stack-theoretic weighted blow-ups on the induced smooth stack-theoretic quotients

X=X_{0}=[X/T_{0}]\leftarrow[X_{1}/T_{1}]\leftarrow\ldots\leftarrow[X_{k}/T_{k}]=[X^{\prime}/T^{\prime}],

such that
{\mathcal{O}}_{[X^{\prime}/T^{\prime}]}\cdot{\mathcal{I}}
is the ideal of an SNC divisor on
[X^{\prime}/T^{\prime}]
.

(8)

The sequence (*) is functorial for smooth morphisms, field extensions, and group actions preserving
({\mathcal{I}},E)
.

1.11.3.
Embedded desingularization

Theorem 1.11.4
.

Let
Y
be a reduced closed subscheme of a smooth scheme
X
over a field
k
of characteristic zero. Let
E
be an SNC divisor on
E
. There exists a sequence of cobordant blow-ups at smooth weighted centers

X=X_{0}\leftarrow X_{1}\leftarrow\ldots\leftarrow X_{k}=X^{\prime}\quad\hfill{(*)}

and the induced sequence of the strict transforms

Y=Y_{0}\leftarrow Y_{1}\leftarrow\ldots\leftarrow Y_{k}=Y^{\prime},

such that

(1)

The torus
T_{i}=G_{m}^{i}
acts on
X_{i}
, with finite stabilizers, where
T_{0}=1
,
T_{i+1}=T_{i}\times G_{m}
and
T:=T_{k}
so that the geometric quotient
X_{i}/T_{i}
exists.

(2)

The closed subschemes
Y_{i}\subset X_{i}
are
T_{i}
-stable. In particular, they admit geometric quotients
Y_{i}/T_{i}
.

(3)

Set
E_{0}:=E
, and let
E_{i}
, for
i\geq 1
, be either the total or the strict transform of
E_{i-1}
. The divisors
E_{i}
on
X_{i}
are
T_{i}
-stable and have SNC.

(4)

The smooth weighted centers of the cobordant blow-ups
X_{i}\leftarrow X_{i+1}
are compatible with
E_{i}
. Moreover, they are
T_{i}
-stable and contained in the locus
{\operatorname{Sing}}_{E_{i}}(Y_{i})
of the points where either
Y_{i}
is singular, or
Y_{i}
is smooth but not transversal to
E_{i}
.

(5)

Y^{\prime}
is a smooth subvariety of
X
and have SNC with
E^{\prime}:=E_{k}
. If
E^{\prime}
is the total transform of
E
then the exceptional divisor of the induced morphism
Y^{\prime}\to Y
is also SNC.

(6)

The sequence (*) defines the sequence of weighted blow-ups on the induced geometric quotients

X=X/T_{0}\leftarrow X_{1}/T_{1}\leftarrow\ldots\leftarrow X_{k}/T_{k}=X^{\prime}/T,
such that
Y^{\prime}/T\subset X^{\prime}/T
admit abelian quotient singularities,

(7)

The sequence (*) defines the sequence of stack- theoretic weighted blow-ups on the induced smooth stack-theoretic quotients

X=X_{0}=[X/T_{0}]\leftarrow[X_{1}/T_{1}]\leftarrow\ldots\leftarrow[X_{k}/T_{k}]=[X^{\prime}/T^{\prime}],

such that
[Y^{\prime}/T^{\prime}]\subset[X^{\prime}/T^{\prime}]
is a smooth substack.

(8)

The sequence (*) is functorial for smooth morphisms, field extensions, and group actions preserving
Y.

Remark 1.11.5
.

The total transform of the divisor
E_{i}
is the union of the strict transforms and the exceptional divisors. Using the total transforms gives better control over the induced exceptional
divisor of the resolution morphism
X^{\prime}\to X
which is SNC and has simple normal crossings with
Y^{\prime}\subset X^{\prime}
. The strict transforms of
E_{i}
give a slightly faster and simpler algorithm without control of the exceptional divisors.

In particular, if
E_{0}=0
and the strict transforms are considered we get
E_{i}=0
, and no divisors are present in the algorithm. In this case we
obtain the resolution procedure similar to the one in
[
ATW19
]
. Moreover, when applying the stack-theoretic quotients as in (5), we reprove the main result of
[
ATW19
, Corollary 1.2.3]
.

1.11.6.
Nonembedded desingularization

Theorem 1.11.7
.

Let
Y
be a reduced scheme of finite type over a field
k
of characteristic zero. There exists a sequence of cobordant blow-ups of regular weighted centers (as in Definition
2.4.7
):

Y=Y_{0}\leftarrow Y_{1}\leftarrow\ldots\leftarrow Y_{k}=Y^{\prime},\quad\hfill{(**)}

such that

(1)

Y^{\prime}
is a smooth variety.

(2)

The torus
T_{i}=G_{m}^{i}
acts on
Y_{i}
, with finite stabilizers, where
T_{0}=1
,
T_{i+1}=T_{i}\times G_{m}
and
T:=T_{k}
, so that the geometric quotient
Y_{i}/T_{i}
exists.

(3)

The inverse image of the exceptional locus is a
T^{\prime}
-stable SNC divisor
E^{\prime}
on
Y^{\prime}

(4)

The inverse image of the nonsingular locus
Y^{ns}\subseteq Y
is equal to
Y^{ns}\times T
, with the natural action of
T
on the second component.

(5)

The sequence (**) defines the sequence of weighted blow-ups on the induced geometric quotients

Y=Y/T_{0}\leftarrow Y_{1}/T_{1}\leftarrow\ldots\leftarrow Y_{k}/T_{k}=Y^{\prime}/T,
such that
Y^{\prime}/T
has an abelian quotient singularities.

(6)

The sequence (**) defines the sequence of stack-theoretic weighted blow-ups on the induced stack-theoretic quotients

Y=Y_{0}=[Y/T_{0}]\leftarrow[Y_{1}/T_{1}]\leftarrow\ldots\leftarrow[Y_{k}/T_{k}]=[Y^{\prime}/T],

such that
[Y^{\prime}/T]
is a smooth stack.

(7)

The sequence (**) is functorial for smooth morphisms, group actions, and field extensions.

2.
Cobordant blow-ups

2.1.
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideals and rational powers of ideals

In
[
ATW19
]
we introduced the notion of the
valuative
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideals
{\mathcal{J}}

or simply
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideals
{\mathcal{J}}
.
In the simplest case they could be directly related to the notion of
rational powers of ideals
considered by Huneke-Swanson (
[
HS06
, Section 10.5]
). They naturally generalize the ideals and can be used for the compact description of the centers. In this paper, for the most part and for computations in characteristic zero, we predominantly focus on the rational Rees algebras.

Definition 2.1.1
.

(
[
ATW19
]
) By a
Q
-ideal
{\mathcal{J}}
on a irreducible noetherian scheme
X
we mean the equivalence classes of the formal expressions
{\mathcal{J}}^{1/n}
, where
{\mathcal{J}}
is the ideal on
X
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
is a natural number. We say that
{\mathcal{J}}^{1/n}
and
{\mathcal{I}}^{1/m}
are
equivalent
if the integral closures of

{\mathcal{J}}^{m}
and
{\mathcal{I}}^{n}
are equal:

({\mathcal{J}}^{m})^{\operatorname{int}}=({\mathcal{I}}^{m})^{\operatorname{int}}.

Remark 2.1.2
.

Note that if
{\mathcal{J}}^{1/n}
and
{\mathcal{I}}^{1/m}
represent the same
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideal for some ideals
{\mathcal{I}}
, and
{\mathcal{J}}
and
m,n\in{\mathbb{N}}
then the ideals
{\mathcal{I}}
and
{\mathcal{J}}
are called
projectively equivalent
wth respect to the coefficient
m/n
. (
[
Rush07
]
)

One can associate with a
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideal
{\mathcal{J}}={\mathcal{I}}^{1/n}
the unique graded Rees algebra

{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathcal{J}}}:=({\mathcal{O}}_{X}[{\mathcal{I}}t^{n}])^{{\operatorname{int}}}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t]

which is the integral closure of

{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[{\mathcal{I}}t^{n}]={\mathcal{O}}_{X}\oplus{\mathcal{I}}t^{n}\oplus{\mathcal{I}}^{2}t^{2n}\oplus\ldots

in
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t]
.
Conversely, it is worth noting that any integrally closed Rees algebra of the form

{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\oplus{\mathcal{I}}_{1}t\oplus\ldots\oplus{\mathcal{I}}_{n}t^{n}\oplus\ldots

can be expressed as the integral closure of
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[{\mathcal{I}}t^{n}]^{{\operatorname{int}}}
for a sufficiently large
n
, where
{\mathcal{I}}={\mathcal{I}}_{n}
. This observation was made, in particular, by Quek:

Proposition 2.1.3
.

[
Que20
, Theorem 2.2.5]
There is a bijective correspondence between the
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideals
{\mathcal{J}}={\mathcal{I}}^{1/n}
on a normal scheme
X
and the integrally closed algebras
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded Rees algebras on
X
, given by

{\mathcal{J}}\mapsto{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathcal{J}}}:={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[{\mathcal{I}}t^{n}]^{{\operatorname{int}}}.

Under this correspondence the ordinary ideal
{\mathcal{I}}
corresponds to
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[{\mathcal{I}}t]^{{\operatorname{int}}}
.∎

With any
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideal
{\mathcal{J}}={\mathcal{I}}^{1/n}
on
X
one can associate
the ideal of sections

({\mathcal{I}}^{1/n})_{X}={\mathcal{J}}_{X}:=\{f\in{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\mid f^{mn}\in{\mathcal{I}}^{m}\mid m\in{\mathbb{N}}\}=\{f\in{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\mid f^{n}\in{\mathcal{I}}^{\operatorname{int}}\mid m\in{\mathbb{N}}\}.

.
It is exactly the
t
-gradation of the algebra
{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathcal{J}}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[{\mathcal{I}}t^{n}]^{{\operatorname{int}}}
.
In particular, if
{\mathcal{J}}={\mathcal{I}}
is an ideal then
{\mathcal{J}}_{X}={\mathcal{I}}^{{\operatorname{int}}}
is it is integral closure.

This terminology is the strictly related to the notion of
rational powers
of ideals which was coined by Huneke-Swanson
[
HS06
, Section 10.5]
.

Definition 2.1.4
.

(
[
HS06
]
) For any ideal
{\mathcal{I}}
and a
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideal
{\mathcal{I}}^{m/n}
for
m,n\in{\mathbb{N}}
the ideal of sections
({\mathcal{I}}^{m/n})_{X}
is called the
m/n
-th
rational power
of ideal
{\mathcal{I}}
.

Using the correspondence between the
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideals and Rees algebras one can associate with the formal sum
\sum^{k}_{i=1}{\mathcal{I}}_{i}^{a_{i}/n_{i}}
, of
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideals
{\mathcal{I}}_{i}^{a_{i}/n_{i}}
on
X
the Rees algebra

{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[{\mathcal{I}}_{1}^{a_{1}}t^{n_{1}},\ldots,{\mathcal{I}}_{k}^{a_{n_{k}}}t^{n_{k}}]^{{\operatorname{int}}},

corresponding to
a certain
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideal on
X
.

This leads to the equality of
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideals:

\sum{\mathcal{I}}_{i}^{1/n}=(\sum{\mathcal{I}}_{i})^{1/n}\quad\mbox{and}\quad{\mathcal{I}}^{1/n}=({\mathcal{I}}^{1/mn})^{m}

and determines the operations of addition and multiplication on the
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideals
on
X
. For any
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideals
{\mathcal{J}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{J}}_{2}
on
X
written in a general form as
{\mathcal{J}}_{1}:=\sum{\mathcal{I}}_{i}^{1/n_{i}}
and
{\mathcal{J}}_{2}:=\sum{\mathcal{J}}_{j}^{1/n_{j}}

their sum is given by

{\mathcal{J}}_{1}+{\mathcal{J}}_{2}=\sum{\mathcal{I}}_{i}^{1/n_{i}}+\sum{\mathcal{J}}_{j}^{1/n_{j}}

and their product by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}_{1}\cdot{\mathcal{J}}_{2}=\sum{\mathcal{I}}_{i}^{1/n_{i}}\cdot\sum{\mathcal{J}}_{j}^{1/n_{j}}=\sum_{i,j}{\mathcal{I}}_{i}^{1/n_{i}}\cdot{\mathcal{J}}_{j}^{1/n_{j}}=

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}({\mathcal{I}}_{i}^{n_{j}}\cdot{\mathcal{J}}_{j}^{n_{i}})^{\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}}=(\sum_{i,j}{\mathcal{I}}_{i}^{n_{j}}\cdot{\mathcal{J}}_{j}^{n_{i}})^{\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}}

and they both define
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideals on
X
.

Note that these operations extend the standard notion of the sum and the product of ideals.
Similarly, we can extend the inclusion relation. Let
{\mathcal{J}}_{1}={\mathcal{I}}_{1}^{1/n_{1}}
, and
{\mathcal{J}}_{2}={\mathcal{I}}_{2}^{1/n_{2}}
be two
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideals on
X
.
We say that
{\mathcal{J}}_{1}\subseteq{\mathcal{J}}_{2}
if we have the inclusion of ideals
({\mathcal{I}}_{1}^{N/n_{1}})^{\operatorname{int}}\subseteq({\mathcal{I}}_{2}^{N/n_{2}})^{\operatorname{int}}
for sufficiently divisible
N
.

2.1.5.
Graded algebras of
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideals

One can associate with the

{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideal
{\mathcal{J}}={\mathcal{I}}^{1/n}
, the corresponding graded algebra of
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideals

{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[{\mathcal{J}}t]:=\bigoplus({\mathcal{J}}^{i})t^{i},

where
t
is a dummy variable. This, in turn, determines the graded Rees algebra of ideals

{\mathcal{A}}_{J}=({\mathcal{O}}_{X}[{\mathcal{J}}t])_{X}:=\bigoplus({\mathcal{J}}^{i})_{X}t^{i}

on
X
.

2.1.6.
Functoriality of
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideals

If
X^{\prime}\to X
is any morphism of integral schemes, and
{\mathcal{I}}^{1/n}
is a
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideal on
X
then
({\mathcal{O}}_{X^{\prime}}\cdot{\mathcal{I}})^{1/n}
is the
preimage of
{\mathcal{I}}^{1/n}
, which is a
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideal on
X^{\prime}
.

2.1.7.
Monomial valuation

Definition 2.1.8
.

Let
u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}
be a partial local system of parameters on a regular irreducible scheme
X
. We say that a valuation
\nu
such that
\nu(u_{i})=w_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
of
K(X)
is
monomial at a point
p\in X
, if

{\mathcal{I}}_{\nu,a,p}:=\{f\in{\mathcal{O}}_{X,p}\mid\nu(f)\geq a\}={\mathcal{O}}_{X,p}\cdot(u_{1}^{a_{1}}\cdot\ldots\cdot u_{k}^{a_{k}}\mid a_{1}w_{1}+\ldots+a_{k}w_{k}\geq a).

Then
\nu
is a
monomial valuation
on
X
if it is monomial at all
p\in V(u_{1},\ldots,u_{k})
.

Lemma 2.1.9
.

Let
X
be a any regular scheme, and
p\in X
be a point.

(1)

If
p\in V(u_{1},\ldots,u_{k})
then assigning weights
w(u_{i}):=w_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
determines a monomial valuation
\nu
on
\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{O}}_{X,p})
with the ideals
{\mathcal{I}}_{\nu,a,p}
as above.

(2)

If
V(u_{1},\ldots,u_{k})
is irreducible on
X
then assigning weights
w(u_{i}):=w_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
determines a unique monomial valuation
\nu
on
X
.

Proof.

(1) We need to show that if
f\in{\mathcal{I}}_{\nu,a,p}\smallsetminus{\mathcal{I}}_{\nu,a+1,p}
and
g\in{\mathcal{I}}_{\nu,b,p}\smallsetminus{\mathcal{I}}_{\nu,b+1,p}
then
fg\in{\mathcal{I}}_{\nu,a+b,p}\smallsetminus{\mathcal{I}}_{\nu,a+b+1,p}
. Suppose otherwise
fg\in{\mathcal{I}}_{\nu,a+b+1,p}
.
By removing the extra terms we can assume that
f=\sum_{\nu(u^{\alpha})=a}c_{\alpha}u^{\alpha}
and
g=\sum_{\nu(u^{\alpha})=b}d_{\alpha}u^{\alpha}
, where all
c_{\alpha},d_{\alpha}
are invertible.
Since
{\mathcal{O}}_{X,p}
is regular then for the ideal
{\mathcal{I}}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{k})
we have that
{\operatorname{gr}}_{I}({\mathcal{O}}_{X_{p}})={\mathcal{O}}_{X_{p}}/({\mathcal{I}})[u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}]
is a domain, and assigning weights
\nu(u_{i})=w_{i}
determines a unique monomial valuation
\nu_{0}
on
{\operatorname{gr}}_{I}({\mathcal{O}}_{X_{p}})={\mathcal{O}}_{X_{p}}/({\mathcal{I}})[u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}]
with the ideals
{\mathcal{I}}_{\nu_{0},a}=\operatorname{in}({\mathcal{I}}_{\nu,a,p})
.
Then considering the initial terms we get
\nu_{0}(\operatorname{in}(fg))=\nu_{0}(\operatorname{in}(f))+\nu_{0}(\operatorname{in}(g))
, which implies that
\operatorname{in}(fg)\not\in{\mathcal{I}}_{\nu_{0},a+b+1}
. But by the assumption
\operatorname{in}(fg))\in\operatorname{in}({\mathcal{I}}_{\nu,a+b+1,p})={\mathcal{I}}_{\nu_{0},a+b+1}
.

(2) The valuation
\nu
is centered at
V(u_{1},\ldots,u_{k})
and uniquely determined for every point. Moreover it is the same when passing to the generic point of
V(u_{1},\ldots,u_{k})
.
∎

2.1.10.
Regular weighted centers

For the purpose of the resolution one considers
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideals on a regular scheme
X
, called
centers
, which can be locally presented in the form
(u_{1}^{a_{1}},\ldots,u_{k}^{a_{k}})
where
u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}
is a partial family of local parameters and
a_{i}\in{\mathbb{Q}}_{>0}
. We shall always assume that
a_{1}\leq\ldots\leq a_{k}
.
Note that one can write it in an equivalent form

(u_{1}^{a_{1}},\ldots,u^{a_{k}})=(u_{1}^{na_{1}},\ldots,u^{na_{k}})^{1/n}
, where
(u_{1}^{na_{1}},\ldots,u^{na_{k}})
is an ordinary ideal for a sufficiently divisible
n
. Then
(u_{1}^{a_{1}},\ldots,u_{k}^{a_{k}})_{X}
will denote the ideal of sections.

One can describe these ideals in a few different equivalent ways.

Lemma 2.1.11
.

(see also
[
ATW19
]
,
[
Que20
]
) Let
X
be a regular
X
scheme.
Let
w_{1}\geq w_{2}\geq\ldots\geq w_{k}
be positive integers, and
u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}
are corresponding local parameters on
X
such that
V(u_{1},\ldots,u_{k})
is irreducible. There is a natural bijective correspondence between

(1)

{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideals
{\mathcal{J}}=(u_{1}^{1/w_{1}},\ldots,u_{k}^{1/w_{k}})
.

(2)

Graded Rees algebras

{\mathcal{A}}_{J}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[u_{1}t^{w_{1}},\ldots,u_{k}t^{w_{k}}]^{\operatorname{int}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[u_{1}t^{c_{1}},\ldots,u_{k}t^{a_{k}}\mid c_{1}\leq w_{1}].

(3)

Monomial valuations
\nu_{J}
, such that
\nu_{J}(u_{i})=w_{i}
, and

{\mathcal{I}}_{\nu_{J},a}:=\{f\in{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\mid\nu_{J}(f)\geq a\}=(u^{\alpha}\mid\nu_{J}(u^{\alpha})\geq a).

Moreover we get

{\mathcal{A}}_{J}=\bigoplus_{a\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}}{\mathcal{I}}_{\nu_{J},a}t^{a}=({\mathcal{O}}_{X}[{\mathcal{J}}\cdot t])_{X}.

First we prove a more explicit form of this correspondence for the centers:

Lemma 2.1.12
.

(see also
[
ATW19
]
,
[
Que20
]
) Let
{\mathcal{J}}=(u_{1}^{1/w_{1}},\ldots,u_{k}^{1/w_{k}})
be a regular center on a regular
X
, where
w_{i}\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Let
\nu_{J}
be a monomial valuation such that
\nu_{J}(u_{i})=w_{i}
, and for any
a>0
,
{\mathcal{I}}_{\nu_{J},a}:=\{f\in{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\mid\nu_{J}(f)\geq a\}
is equal to
(u^{\alpha}\mid\nu_{J}(u^{\alpha})\geq a)
. Then for any
a\in{\mathbb{Q}}_{>0}
:

((u_{1}^{1/w_{1}},\ldots,u_{k}^{1/w_{k}})^{a})_{X}={\mathcal{I}}_{\nu_{J},a}=\{f\in{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\mid\nu_{J}(f)\geq a\}=

(u_{1}^{c_{1}}\cdot\ldots\cdot u_{k}^{c_{k}}\mid c_{i}\in{\mathbb{N}},\,\,c_{1}w_{1}+\ldots c_{k}w_{k}\geq a).

In particular

((u_{1}^{a_{1}},\ldots,u_{k}^{a_{k}}))_{X}=(u_{1}^{c_{1}}\cdot\ldots\cdot u_{k}^{c_{k}}\mid\,\,c_{i}\in{\mathbb{N}},\,c_{1}/a_{1}+\ldots+c_{k}/a_{k}\geq 1).

Proof.

Note that for any monomial
u^{c}=u_{1}^{c_{1}}\cdot\ldots\cdot u_{1}^{c_{k}}
with integral
c_{i}
and for
a\in{\mathbb{Q}}_{>0}
we have :

u^{c}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{\nu_{J},a}\,\,\Leftrightarrow\,\,c_{1}w_{1}+\ldots+c_{k}w_{k}\geq a.

Let
n
be now any positive integer which is divisible by
{\rm lcm}(w_{1},\ldots,w_{k})
, and write
a_{i}w_{i}=n
, for
the relevant integers
a_{i}
. Then, the power
(u^{c})^{n}=(u_{1}^{a_{1}})^{c_{1}w_{1}}\cdot\ldots\cdot(u_{k}^{a_{k}})^{c_{k}w_{k}}
is an element of an (ordinary) ideal
(u_{1}^{a_{1}},\ldots,u_{k}^{a_{k}})^{n}
if and only if
c_{1}w_{1}+\ldots+c_{k}w_{1}\geq n
.

Thus if
u^{c}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{\nu_{J},n}
then
c_{1}w_{1}+\ldots+c_{k}w_{k}\geq n
and
(u^{c})^{n}\in(u_{1}^{a_{1}},\ldots,u_{k}^{a_{k}})^{n}
, whence

u^{c}\in((u_{1}^{a_{1}},\ldots,u_{k}^{a_{k}})^{int}=(u_{1}^{a_{1}},\ldots,u_{k}^{a_{k}})_{X}
.
This shows that
{\mathcal{I}}_{\nu_{J},n}\subseteq((u_{1}^{a_{1}},\ldots,u_{k}^{a_{k}})_{X}
.
On the other hand
(u_{1}^{a_{1}},\ldots,u_{k}^{a_{k}})\subseteq{\mathcal{I}}_{\nu_{J},n}
, and since
{\mathcal{I}}_{\nu_{J},n}
is integrally closed
(u_{1}^{a_{1}},\ldots,u_{k}^{a_{k}})^{int}\subseteq{\mathcal{I}}_{\nu_{J},n}
.
This gives
{\mathcal{I}}_{\nu_{J},n}=(u_{1}^{a_{1}},\ldots,u_{k}^{a_{k}})_{X}
, for any integral
n
, and
a_{i}
such that
n=a_{i}w_{i}
.
In general, let
a=\frac{l}{m}\in{\mathbb{Q}}_{>0}
, with
l,m
integral, and
and let
n
be as above with
n=a_{i}w_{i}
. Then, by the definition, and the above
f\in((u_{1}^{1/w_{1}},\ldots,u_{k}^{1/w_{k}})^{a})_{X}
if and only if
f^{mn}\in((u_{1}^{a_{1}},\ldots,u_{k}^{a_{k}})^{l})_{X}={\mathcal{I}}_{\nu_{J},l}
, which is equivalent to
\nu_{J}(f^{mn})\geq ln\Leftrightarrow\nu_{J}(f)\geq a.

For the second part, consider a sufficiently divisible
integer
a>0
such that

w_{i}:=a/a_{i}
are integral. Then we can write

(u_{1}^{a_{1}},\ldots,u_{k}^{a_{k}})=(u_{1}^{1/w_{1}},\ldots,u_{k}^{1/w_{k}})^{a}

Thus, by the previous part
u^{c}\in(u_{1}^{a_{1}},\ldots,u_{k}^{a_{k}})_{X}
is equivalent to

c_{1}w_{1}+\ldots c_{k}w_{k}\geq a
, which translates into
c_{1}/a_{1}+\ldots+c_{k}/a_{k}\geq 1
.

∎

Proof.

(1)\Leftrightarrow(3)
By Lemma
2.1.12
, we can write
the Rees algebra
({\mathcal{O}}_{X}[{\mathcal{J}}t])_{X}
as
({\mathcal{O}}_{X}[{\mathcal{J}}t])_{X}=\bigoplus_{a\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}}{\mathcal{I}}_{\nu_{J},a}t^{a}
. Thus the valuation
{\nu_{J}}
is determined uniquely. Conversely given a monomial valuation
\nu
with the weights
w_{1},\ldots,w_{k}
one can recover a
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideal
{\mathcal{J}}
from
N
-th gradation
({\mathcal{J}}^{N})_{X}={{\mathcal{I}}_{\nu_{J},N}}
of
({\mathcal{O}}_{X}[{\mathcal{J}}t])_{X}
for
N=w_{1}\cdot\ldots\cdot w_{k}
as
{\mathcal{J}}=({\mathcal{I}}_{\nu_{J},N})^{1/N}
.

(2)\Leftrightarrow(3)

Given a monomial valuation
\nu_{J}
, one can form the integrally closed algebra

{\mathcal{A}}_{J}:=\bigoplus_{a\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}}{\mathcal{I}}_{\nu_{J},a}t^{a}=\{f\cdot t^{a}\mid f\in{\mathcal{O}}_{X},\mid a\leq{\nu_{J}(f)}\}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[u_{1}t^{c_{1}},\ldots,u_{k}t^{c_{k}}\mid c_{1}\leq w_{1}].

Conversely, any algebra
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[u_{1}t^{c_{1}},\ldots,u_{k}t^{c_{k}}\mid c_{1}\leq w_{1}]
determines a monomial valuation defined as follows: For any
f\in{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
,
we put
\nu_{J}(f)=\max\{a:t^{a}\cdot f\in{\mathcal{A}}_{J}\}
.

∎

2.1.13.
Blow-ups of
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideals on
X

Any
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideal of the form
{\mathcal{J}}={\mathcal{I}}^{1/n}
on a normal scheme
X
determines a unique blow-up

Y={{\mathcal{P}}roj}({\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{J}})\to X

of
{\mathcal{J}}
on
X
which can be understood as the normalized blow-up of
{\mathcal{I}}
on
X
. It transforms
{\mathcal{J}}={\mathcal{I}}^{1/n}
into a

({\mathcal{O}}_{X}\cdot{\mathcal{I}})^{1/n}=({\mathcal{O}}_{X}(-E))^{1/n}
for a Cartier exceptional divisor
E
.

2.1.14.
Weighted blow-ups of centers

In particular, the center
{\mathcal{J}}=(x_{1}^{1/w_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}^{1/w_{k}})
on a regular scheme
X
determines the weighted blow-up

Y={{\mathcal{P}}roj}({\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{J}})={{\mathcal{P}}roj}({{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}[t^{w_{1}}{x_{1}},\ldots,t^{w_{k}}{x_{k}}]^{\rm int}).

2.1.15.
Stack-theoretic weighted blow-ups

The above formulas of the standard blow-up can be natural extended to the stack-theoretic setting.
In
[
ATW19
, Section 3.1]
the stack-theoretic blow-up of any
{\mathbb{Q}}
- ideal

{\mathcal{J}}
is defined to be the stack-theoretic quotient of the scheme
(using our notation):

[(\operatorname{Spec}_{X}({{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[{\mathcal{J}}t])_{X}\smallsetminus V(({\mathcal{J}}t)_{X}))/G_{m}]},

with the natural action of the multiplicative group
G_{m}:=\operatorname{Spec}{k[t,t^{-1}]}
.

In particular, the
stack-theoretic weighted blow-up of

{\mathcal{J}}=(u_{1}^{1/w_{1}},\ldots,u_{k}^{1/w_{k}})
is defined to be the stack-theoretic quotient

\displaystyle=[(\operatorname{Spec}_{X}({{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}[t^{w_{1}}{x_{1}},\ldots,t^{w_{k}}{x_{k}}]^{\rm int})\smallsetminus V(t^{w_{1}}{x_{1}},\ldots,t^{w_{k}}{x_{k}}))/G_{m}]=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[(\operatorname{Spec}_{X}({{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}[t^{a_{1}}{x_{1}},\ldots,t^{a_{k}}{x_{k}}\mid c_{i}\leq w_{i}])\smallsetminus V(t^{w_{1}}{x_{1}},\ldots,t^{w_{k}}{x_{k}})/G_{m}].

Observe that the space
\operatorname{Spec}_{X}({{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}[t^{a_{1}}{x_{1}},\ldots,t^{a_{k}}{x_{k}}\mid c_{i}\leq w_{i}])
is usually non regular even for an ordinary blow-up of a regular center.

2.2.
Rational Rees algebra

In this paper we are going to consider Rees algebras on a scheme
X
with gradations given by a finitely generated additive
subsemigoups
\Gamma
of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{\geq 0}
.

Definition 2.2.1
.

By a
rational Rees algebra
or simply
Rees algebra
we mean a finitely generated
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-algebra which can be written of the form:

R=\bigoplus_{a\in\Gamma}R_{a}t^{a}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{1/w_{R}}],

where
w_{R}\in{\mathbb{Q}}_{>0}
is the smallest rational number such that
\Gamma\subseteq(1/w_{R})\cdot{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
, and the ideals
R_{a}\subseteq{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
satisfy

(1)

R_{0}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}

(2)

R_{a}\cdot R_{b}\subseteq R_{a+b}

If
R
is a rational Rees algebra on
X
, and
w
is a multiple of
w_{R}
then
R^{{\operatorname{ext}}}:=R[t^{-1/w}]
will be called an
extended Rees algebra
.

By the
integral closure
R^{\operatorname{int}}
of a rational Rees algebra
R=\bigoplus_{a\in\Gamma}R_{a}t^{a}
we shall mean its integral closure in
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{1/w_{R}}]
. The integral closure in
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{1/w}]
, where
w=nw_{R}
is a relevant integral multiple of
w_{R}
will be denoted by
R^{\operatorname{Int}}
.

By the
vertex

V(R)
of
R
(or
V(R^{{\operatorname{ext}}})
of
R^{{\operatorname{ext}}}
) we mean the vanishing locus

V(R)=V(\sum_{a>0}R_{a})

(respectively
V(R^{{\operatorname{ext}}})=V(\sum_{a>0}R^{\operatorname{ext}}_{a})).

Remark 2.2.2
.

We do not assume here that
R_{a}\subseteq R_{b}
if
a\geq b
for
a,b\in\Gamma
. However this condition is satisfied if

R=R^{\operatorname{int}}
is integrally closed in
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{1/w_{R}}]
.

2.2.3.
Rational Rees algebras and ideals

With any ideal
{\mathcal{I}}
one associates the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded Rees algebra

{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{I}}:={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[{\mathcal{I}}t]=\bigoplus{\mathcal{I}}^{n}t^{n}
, and the extended Rees algebra

{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{I}}^{\operatorname{ext}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{-1},{\mathcal{I}}t].

As we mentioned before the main idea of the rational Rees algebras is to enlarge the gradation so we obtain a simpler presentation of the graded algebra, and thus a nicer presentation of the blow-up of
{\mathcal{I}}
.

2.2.4.
Rees centers and
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideals

By a
Rees center
we mean a Rees algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
locally of the form

{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[x_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{\operatorname{int}},

for some a local partial system of coordinates( local sytem of parameters)
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
and some positive rational numbers
a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}
, where the integral closure is considered in
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{1/w_{A}}]
, where
w_{A}:={\operatorname{lcm}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})
is the smallest positive rational such that
w_{A}/a_{1},\ldots,{w_{A}/a_{k}}
are all integers.

By the
extended center
which is also called
center
we shall mean the extended Rees algebra

{\mathcal{A}}^{\operatorname{ext}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{-1/w},x_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}],

where
w
is a multiple of
w_{A}
, so
w/a_{i}
are all positive integers.

2.2.5.
Rescaling

Definition 2.2.6
.

Given
w_{0}\in{\mathbb{Q}}_{>0}
, by the
t\mapsto t^{w_{0}}
rescaling
of the rational Rees algebra
R=\bigoplus_{a\in\Gamma}R_{a}t^{a}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{1/w_{R}}]

we mean the Rees algebra

R^{w_{0}}=\bigoplus_{a\in\Gamma}R_{a}t^{w_{0}a}\subseteq{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{w_{0}/w_{R}}],

Lemma 2.2.7
.

There is a natural isomorphism
R\to R^{w_{0}}

ft^{a}\mapsto ft^{w_{0}a}
. In particular,
R
is integrally closed in
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{1/w_{R}}]
iff
R^{w}
is integrally closed in
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{w_{0}/w_{R}}]
. ∎

Lemma 2.2.8
.

Let

{\mathcal{A}}:={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{\operatorname{int}}
and

{\mathcal{A}}^{\operatorname{ext}}:={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{-1/w},\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}],

where

w
is a multiple of
w_{A}
. Then
the integral closure
{\mathcal{A}}^{\operatorname{Int}}
of
{\mathcal{A}}
in
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{1/w}]
is equal to

{\mathcal{A}}^{\operatorname{Int}}:={\mathcal{A}}^{\operatorname{ext}}_{\geq 0}
.

Moreover if
V({\mathcal{A}})
is irreducible then there is a unique monomial associated valuation
\nu_{A}
such that
\nu_{A}(x_{i})=1/a_{i}
, and

{\mathcal{A}}_{a}=\{f\in{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\mid\nu_{A}(f)\geq a\}.

Proof.

It is a direct consequence of Lemma
2.1.11
, obtained by rescaling
t\mapsto t^{1/w}
.

∎

2.3.
Regular centers vs Rees centers

One can identify a regular
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideal center locally described by
{\mathcal{J}}=(u_{1}^{1/w_{1}},\ldots,u_{k}^{1/w_{k}})
with
w_{i}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, with the Rees center

{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{J}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[u_{1}t^{w_{1}},\ldots,u_{k}t^{w_{k}}]^{\operatorname{int}}
, or the extended Rees center

{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{J}}^{\operatorname{ext}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{-1},u_{1}t^{w_{1}},\ldots,u_{k}t^{w_{k}}].

Per analogy with the correspondence
{\mathcal{J}}\mapsto{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{J}}

we associate with arbitrary
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideal
{\mathcal{J}}=(x_{1}^{a_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}^{a_{k}})
, with positive rational
a_{i}
the rational Rees algebra

{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[x_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{\operatorname{int}},

and the extended center

{\mathcal{A}}^{\operatorname{ext}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{-1/w},x_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}],

where
w={\operatorname{lcm}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})
.

Conversely any extended center
{\mathcal{A}}^{\operatorname{ext}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{-1/w},x_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}],
with
{\operatorname{lcm}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})|w
determines a
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideal
{\mathcal{J}}=(u_{1}^{a_{1}/w},\ldots,u_{k}^{a_{k}/w})
, with

{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{J}}=({\mathcal{A}}^{\operatorname{ext}})^{w}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{-1},x_{1}t^{w/a_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{w/a_{k}}].

Thus there is a bijective correspondence between

•

{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideals centers
{\mathcal{J}}=(u_{1}^{1/w_{1}},\ldots,u_{k}^{1/w_{k}})
with
w_{i}\in{\mathbb{N}}

•

the extended integral Rees algebras
{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathcal{J}}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{-1},x_{1}t^{w_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{w_{k}}],
and

•

the extended rational Rees algebras
{\mathcal{A}}^{\operatorname{ext}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{-1/w},x_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}],
with
w_{i}=w/a_{i}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, defined up to rescaling.

2.4.
Cobordant blow-ups

2.4.1.
Good and geometric quotient

We consider here a relatively affine action of
T=\operatorname{Spec}({\mathbb{Z}}[t_{1},t_{1}^{-1},\ldots,t_{k},t_{k}^{-1}])
on a scheme
X
over
{\mathbb{Z}}
. By the
good quotient (or GIT quotient)
we mean an affine
T
-invariant morphism
\pi:X\to Y=X\sslash T
such that the induced morphism of the sheaves
{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}\to\pi_{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})
defines the isomorphism onto the subsheaf of invariants
{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}\simeq\pi_{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})^{T}
.

Then
\pi:X\to Y=X/T
will be called a
geometric quotient
if additionally every fiber
X_{\overline{y}}
of
\pi
over a geometric point
\overline{y}:\operatorname{Spec}(\overline{k})\to Y
defines a single orbit of the action of
T_{\overline{k}}=T\times_{k}\operatorname{Spec}(\overline{k})=\operatorname{Spec}(\overline{k}[t_{1},t_{1}^{-1},\ldots,t_{k},t_{k}^{-1}])
on
X_{\overline{y}}
.

2.4.2.
Birational cobordisms

Since the space

\operatorname{Spec}_{X}({{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}[t^{w_{1}}{x_{1}},\ldots,t^{w_{k}}{x_{k}}]^{\rm int})=\\
\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{A}}_{J})
in the definition of the stack-theoretic quotient is singular even for the standard smooth blow-up with weights
w_{i}=1
we consider here an alternative approach which uses the concept of
birational cobordisms
. The notion was developed over a field, but it can be extended to the schemes with the action of
the group scheme
G_{m}:=\operatorname{Spec}({\mathbb{Z}}[t,t^{-1}]
. One shall mention that the theory of cobordisms was also considered in the language of schemes in
[
AT19
, Definition 4.3]
, but their definition of cobordism is far more restrictive.

The following definition is motivated for the analogous definition for the varieties with
G_{m}
-action and is adapted to the schemes.

Definition 2.4.3
.

Let
X
be an integral scheme with the action of

G_{m}
. Let
p\in X
be a point.
We say that
\lim_{t\to 0}(tp)
exists
,
(respectively
\lim_{t\to\infty}(tp)
exists
) if there is an open neighborhood
U
of
p
such that the morphism
G_{m}\times U\to X

extends to the
\operatorname{Spec}({\mathbb{Z}}[t])\times U\to X

(respectively to
\operatorname{Spec}({\mathbb{Z}}[t^{-1}])\times U\to X
).

The following definition extends the original definition over a field.

Definition 2.4.4
.

[
Wło00
, Definition 2]
By a birational cobordism representing a birational map
\phi:X_{1}\dashrightarrow X_{2}
of schemes we mean a scheme with an action of
T=G_{m}=\operatorname{Spec}({\mathbb{Z}}[t,t^{-1}])
such that the sets

\begin{array}[]{rcrl}B_{-}&:=&\{p\in B:&\lim_{t\to 0\ }(tp)\mbox{ does not exist in }B\}\\
\mbox{and }B_{+}&:=&\{x\in B:&\lim_{t\to\infty}(tp)\mbox{ does not exist
in }B\}\end{array}

are nonempty Zariski open subsets of
B
, and the geometric quotients
\alpha_{1}:B_{+}/T\simeq X_{1}
, and
\alpha_{2}:B_{-}/T\simeq X_{2}
exists, with the natural birational
map
\psi:B_{+}/T\to B_{-}/T
defined by open inclusions
maps
(B_{-}\cap B_{+})/T\to B_{\pm}/T
, such that

\phi\circ\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}\circ\psi.

Example 2.4.5
.

[
Wło00
, Example 2]
Let
T
act on
B={\mathbf{A}}_{k}^{n+1}=\operatorname{Spec}(k[x_{0},\ldots,x_{n}])
by

t(x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=(t^{-1}x_{0},t^{w_{1}}x_{1},\ldots,t^{w_{k}}x_{k})
, where
w_{1}\geq w_{2}\geq\ldots\geq w_{k}>0
.
Then
B_{-}=B\smallsetminus V(x_{0})
,
B_{+}=B\smallsetminus V(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
.

By using toric geometry, we will see that the induced morphism
B_{+}/T\to B_{-}/T=B\sslash T
is the weighted blow-up at
{\mathcal{J}}=(u_{1}^{1/w_{1}},\ldots,u_{k}^{1/w_{k}})
.

First, the cobordism
B
corresponds to the regular cone

\sigma=\langle e_{0},\ldots,e_{n}\rangle:=\sum_{i=0}^{n}{\mathbb{Q}}e_{i}

in
{\mathbb{Q}}^{n+1}
with the basis
\{e_{0},\ldots,e_{n}\}
.
The action of
T
is determined by the vector
v:=-e_{0}+\sum w_{i}e_{i}
, representing the weights.
Then the good quotient

B\to B\sslash T=\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{O}}(B)^{T})

corresponds to the map
\sigma\to\pi(\sigma)=\langle e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}\rangle
, defined by
the projection

\pi:{\mathbb{Q}}^{n+1}\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{n+1}/{\rm span}(v)\simeq{\mathbb{Q}}^{n}

where

{\mathbb{Q}}^{n}
is spanned by
\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}\}
. It can be described as
\pi(x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=(x_{1}+w_{1},\ldots,x_{n}+w_{n})
. Note that face
\sigma_{0}:=\langle e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}\rangle
of
\sigma
can be identified with its image
\pi(\sigma_{0})=\pi(\sigma)
.

The cone
\sigma
with a vector
v
and the projection
\pi
is an example of the
combinatorial cobordism
in the sense of Morelli
[
Mor96
]
.
The ”lower boundary”
B_{-}
of
B
corresponds to the ”lower boundary”
\sigma_{-}
of
\sigma
, which is the complex consisting of the faces of
\sigma
visible from below with respect to
v
. It contains
\sigma_{0}
and all of its faces. The geometric quotient
B_{-}\to B_{-}/T
corresponds to the restriction
\sigma_{0}\to\pi(\sigma_{0})=\pi(\sigma)
of
\pi:{\mathbb{Q}}^{n+1}\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{n}
mapping isomorphically
\sigma_{0}
to
\pi(\sigma_{0})
.
So both quotients: the geometric quotient
B_{-}/T
and the good quotient
B\sslash T=\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{O}}(B))^{T}
can be identified as they correspond to
\pi(\sigma_{0})=\pi(\sigma)=\sigma_{0}\subset{\mathbb{Q}}^{n}
.

The ”upper boundary”
B_{+}
corresponds to the subcomplex
\sigma_{+}
with the maximal
faces
\langle e_{0},e_{1},\ldots,\check{e_{i}},\ldots,e_{n}\rangle
, for
i=1,\ldots,n
, ”visible from above”. Their projection
\pi(\sigma_{+})
corresponds to the star subdivision of
\sigma_{0}
at the vector
w:=\pi(e_{0})=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{n})
with maximal faces
\langle w,e_{1},\ldots,\check{e_{i}},\ldots,e_{n}\rangle
.

Consequently, the morphism

B_{+}/T\to B_{-}/T=B\sslash T=\operatorname{Spec}(k[x_{0},\ldots,x_{k}])^{T}=\operatorname{Spec}(k[u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}]),

where
u_{i}:=x_{i}x_{0}^{w_{i}}
,
is the weighted blow-up corresponding to the star subdivision
\pi(\sigma_{+})
of
\pi(\sigma)=\sigma_{0}
.
The
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideal
{\mathcal{J}}=(u_{1}^{1/w_{1}},\ldots,u_{k}^{1/w_{k}})
defines a piecewise linear convex function

F_{\mathcal{I}}:=\min\{(1/w_{i})\cdot x_{i}\mid i=1,\ldots,n\}

on
\sigma_{0}
such that
F_{\mathcal{I}}(e_{i})=0
, and
F_{\mathcal{I}}(w)=1
, and which is linear exactly on the faces
\langle w,e_{1},\ldots,\check{e_{i}},\ldots,e_{n}\rangle
. Thus we see that the blow-up of
{\mathcal{J}}=(u_{1}^{1/w_{1}},\ldots,u_{k}^{1/w_{k}})
corresponds to the star subdivision of
\sigma
at
v
, and can be identified with
B_{+}/T\to B_{-}/T=B\sslash T
.

One can describe the morphism
B_{+}/T\to B_{-}/T=B\sslash T
explicitly. Set
t:=x_{0}^{-1}
. Then we can write

{\mathcal{B}}_{J}:={\mathcal{O}}(B)=k[x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}]=k[t^{-1},t^{w_{1}}u_{1},\ldots,t^{w_{k}}u_{k}]\quad\mbox{and}

\displaystyle B_{+}/T=(\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{J}})\smallsetminus V(t^{w_{1}}u_{1},\ldots,t^{w_{k}}u_{k}))/T\to

\displaystyle\to\quad B/T=B_{-}/T=\operatorname{Spec}(k[u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}]).

Moreover the algebra

{\mathcal{A}}_{J}=(k[u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}])[T^{1/a_{1}}u_{1},\ldots,t^{a_{k}}u_{k}\mid a_{i}\leq w_{i}]=(k[t^{-1},t^{w_{1}}u_{1},\ldots,t^{w_{k}}u_{k}])_{\geq 0}

is exactly the nonnegative part of the algebra
B_{J}
. Thus
the natural homomorphism
A_{J}\subset B_{J}
defines the isomorphisms of the localizations
(A_{J})_{t^{w_{i}}u_{i}}\to(B_{J})_{t^{w_{i}}u_{i}}

which gives the isomorphism of quasi-affine schemes

\operatorname{Spec}(B_{J})\smallsetminus V(t^{w_{1}}u_{1},\ldots,t^{w_{k}}u_{k})\to\operatorname{Spec}(A_{J})\smallsetminus V(t^{w_{1}}u_{1},\ldots,t^{w_{k}}u_{k}),

and their quotients
B_{+}/T\to Proj_{X}({\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{J}})
over
B/T
.

2.4.6.
Cobordant blow-ups

The idea of
cobordant blow-up
is to represent a weighted blow-up by a smooth cobordism.

Let
T:={\mathbb{G}}_{m}=Spec({\mathbb{Z}}[t,t^{-1}])
.

Definition 2.4.7
.

Let
X
be a scheme.
A trivial cobordant blow-up
of
X
is simply the natural projection
X\times T\to X
from the product
X\times T=\operatorname{Spec}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t,t^{-1}]
.

By the
full cobordant blow-up
of
X
at an extended Rees center

{\mathcal{A}}^{\operatorname{ext}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{-1/w},x_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]

we mean the
T
-invariant morphism
\sigma:B\to X
defined by the birational cobordism

B:=\operatorname{Spec}_{X}(({\mathcal{A}}^{\operatorname{ext}})^{w})=\operatorname{Spec}_{X}({{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}[t^{-1},t^{w_{1}}{x_{1}},\ldots,t^{w_{k}}{x_{k}}]),

where
w={\operatorname{lcm}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})
, and
w_{i}=w/a_{i}
.

The upper and lower boundaries are equal to

B_{+}=B\smallsetminus V(({\mathcal{A}}^{\operatorname{ext}}))=B\smallsetminus V(t^{w_{1}}{x_{1}},\ldots,t^{w_{k}}{x_{k}})=B\smallsetminus{\operatorname{Vert}}(B)

B_{-}=B\smallsetminus V(t^{-1})=\operatorname{Spec}_{X}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t,t^{-1}]).

By the
cobordant blow-up
we mean the
T
-invariant morphism
\sigma_{+}:B_{+}\to X
. Here
\sigma_{-}:B_{-}\to X
is the trivial cobordant blow-up.

Equivalently
B
,
B_{+}
will be called
full cobordant blow-up
(resp.
cobordant blow-up
)
of a
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideal center

{\mathcal{J}}=(x_{1}^{1/w_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}^{1/w_{k}})
.

We shall call the closed subscheme

V={\operatorname{Vert}}(B):=V(t^{w_{1}}{x_{1}},\ldots,t^{w_{k}}{x_{k}})=V(({\mathcal{A}}^{\operatorname{ext}})^{w})=V({\mathcal{O}}_{B}\cdot{\mathcal{J}})=B\smallsetminus B_{+}

the
vertex
of
B
(per analogy to the vertex of the affine cone over a projective scheme.)

Thus, as we shall see
B
is in fact the birational cobordism representing the weighted blow-up of
{\mathcal{J}}
, with
B_{+}=B\smallsetminus V
, and
B_{-}=B\smallsetminus D=B\times G_{m}
, where
D:=V_{B}(t^{-1})
is the exceptional divisor and such that

B_{+}/\penalty G_{m}=B\sslash G_{m}={{\mathcal{P}}roj}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{w_{1}}{x_{1}},\ldots,t^{w_{k}}{x_{k}}]^{\operatorname{int}}),

and
B_{-}/\penalty G_{m}=X
.

Remark 2.4.8
.

The author learned recently from Dan Abramovich that the algebras
{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}[t^{-1},t^{w_{1}}{x_{1}},\ldots,t^{w_{k}}{x_{k}}]
were also considered in the context of stack-theoretic blow-ups by Quek and Rydh in
their upcoming paper
[
QR22
]
. In fact, Rydh noticed independently that such algebras gives a nice and natural description of stack-theoretic weighted blow-ups, which was part of his original goal for
[
QR22
]
. On the other hand, cobordant blow-ups are a different point of view on the same algebra, and they are motivated by the interpretation of the weighted blow-ups in terms of torus action and smooth birational cobordisms to avoid the use of the Artin stacks in positive and mixed characteristic.
The cobordant blow-ups and the full cobordant blow-ups carry additional useful information and are often simpler for computations.
One shall mention that all these ideas stem primarily from the paper
[
ATW19
]
.

On the other hand, one can link this definition to the
extended Rees algebras

{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{-1},{\mathcal{I}}t]
introduced by Rees and considered by Swanson and Huneke in
[
HS06
, Definition 5.1]
, in particular, in the context of blow-ups of smooth centers.

Remark 2.4.9
.

(1)

By Lemma
5.2.6
, the geometric quotient
B_{+}/{\mathbb{G}}_{m}\to X
exists. Moreover, by recalling the considerations in the second part of Example
2.4.5
, it can be naturally identified with the usual weighted blow-up:

\displaystyle B_{+}/{\mathbb{G}}_{m}\simeq\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{-1},t^{w_{1}}{x_{1}},\ldots,t^{w_{k}}{x_{k}}]\smallsetminus V(t^{w_{1}}{x_{1}},\ldots,t^{w_{k}}{x_{k}})))/T\simeq

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{P}}roj}({\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathcal{J}}})\simeq({{\mathcal{P}}roj}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{w_{1}}{x_{1}},\ldots,t^{w_{k}}{x_{k}}]))^{nor}\to X=B\sslash{\mathbb{G}}_{m}=B_{-}/{\mathbb{G}}_{m}.

(2)

The stack-theoretic quotient of smooth varieties over a field
k
of characteristic zero
[B_{+}/{\mathbb{G}}_{m}]\to X
is equivalent to the definition considered in
[
ATW19
, Section 3.1]
.

(3)

Similarly to the standard blow-up, the cobordant blow-up is trivial over the complement of the geometric locus of the center
V({\mathcal{J}})=V(x_{1}\ldots,x_{k})
:

\sigma_{\pm}:B_{+}\cap B_{-}=\sigma^{-1}(X\smallsetminus V({\mathcal{J}}))\simeq(X\smallsetminus V({\mathcal{J}}))\times T\to X\smallsetminus V({\mathcal{J}}).

(4)

The vertex
V={\operatorname{Vert}}(B)
plays a crucial role in the resolution process. It typically represents the locus of points on
B
that exhibit the most severe singularities. The singularities at
{\operatorname{Vert}}(B)
usually coincide with the singularities along the center
\mathcal{J}
and are eliminated when transitioning to
B_{+}
.

2.5.
Exceptional divisor

The following result explains the definition of the
exceptional divisor
D:=V_{B}(t^{-1})
in the language of
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideals:

Lemma 2.5.1
.

The cobordant blow-up
B_{+}
transforms the center
{{\mathcal{J}}}=(x_{1}^{\frac{1}{w_{1}}},\ldots,x_{k}^{\frac{1}{w_{k}}})

into the (ordinary) ideal of the exceptional divisor
D
on
B_{+}
generated by a global invariant parameter
t^{-1}
:

{\mathcal{I}}_{D}:={{\mathcal{J}}}\cdot{\mathcal{O}}_{B_{+}}=t^{-1}{\mathcal{O}}_{B_{+}}

Proof.

We have

{\mathcal{J}}\cdot{\mathcal{O}}_{B_{+}}=({\mathcal{J}}\cdot t)t^{-1}\cdot{\mathcal{O}}_{B_{+}}

Note however that

({\mathcal{J}}\cdot t)=((x_{1}t^{w_{1}})^{1/w_{1}},\ldots,(x_{k}t^{w_{k}})^{1/w_{k}})={\mathcal{O}}_{B_{+}}

is a trivial
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideal on
B_{+}
.
∎

2.5.2.
Cobordant blow-up: local equations

Let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
be a system of local parameters (or coordinates) at a point
p
on a regular
X
, and let
{\mathcal{J}}=(x_{1}^{1/w_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}^{1/w_{k}})
be a center. Then the full cobordant blow-up
B\to X
of
{\mathcal{J}}
, can be represented as
