B=\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{-1},x^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{n}]/((x^{\prime}_{1}t^{-w_{1}}-x_{1}),\ldots,(x^{\prime}_{k}t^{-w_{1}}-x_{k})].

Thus
B\subset X\times{\mathbb{A}}^{n+1}
is locally a closed regular subscheme of
X\times{\mathbb{A}}^{n+1}
.
Moreover it has system of local
T
-semiinvariant parameters
t^{-1},x^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{k},x_{k+1},\ldots,x_{n}
at the relevant
p^{\prime}\in V(t^{-1},x^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{k},x_{k+1}\ldots,x_{n})
mapping to
p
.
Consequently, the full cobordant blow-up
B\to X
at
(x_{1}^{1/w_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}^{1/w_{k}})
can be described by a single chart with the following coordinates:

•

t^{-1}
is the inverse of the coordinate
t
representing the action of torus
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}=\operatorname{Spec}({\mathbb{Z}}[t,t^{-1}])
.

•

x_{i}^{\prime}=x_{i}\cdot t^{w_{i}}
for
1\leq i\leq k
, and

•

x_{j}^{\prime}=x_{j}
for
j>k
.

Remark 2.5.3
.

For some computations and considerations it is more natural to replace
t^{-1}
with
s
so that
x_{i}^{\prime}=x_{i}/s^{w_{i}}
in the above formulas. On the other hand the construction of the center and the majority of the computations are consistent with the graded Rees algebras and thus with our notation.

The cobordant blow-up
B_{+}=B\smallsetminus V(x^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{k})
can be covered by the open subsets
(B_{+})_{x^{\prime}_{i}}=B\smallsetminus V(x^{\prime}_{i})
, associated with
x^{\prime}_{i}
producing several more specific ”charts” similarly to the standard blow-up.

2.5.4.
Cobordant blow-ups of toric varieties

By definition, the full cobordant blow-up of a smooth toric variety at a weighted toric center is again a smooth toric variety.
Let
X_{\sigma}=\operatorname{Spec}k[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
be the affine toric variety associated with
\sigma\subset N_{R}:={\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{n}
. The full cobordant blow-up
B=X_{\tau}\to X_{\sigma}
of the
{\mathbb{Q}}
ideal
(x_{1}^{1/w_{1}},\ldots,x_{n}^{1/w_{n}})

is the morphism of toric varieties corresponding to the inclusion of algebras:

k[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]\subseteq k[x_{1}t^{w_{1}},\ldots,x_{n}t^{w_{n}},t^{-1}]

The standard weighted blow-up at
{\mathcal{J}}

corresponds to the star subdivision of
\sigma
with the center
v:=w_{1}e_{1}+\ldots+w_{n}e_{n}
. The full cobordant blow-up
B
is described by the regular cone
\tau:=\langle e_{1},\ldots,e_{n},v+e_{n+1}\rangle

with the natural projection onto
\sigma=\langle e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}\rangle
along the vector
e_{n+1}
.
The cone
\tau
is a cobordism in the sense of Morelli
[
Mor96
]
.
The upper
boundary
\tau_{+}
of
\tau
has maximal faces
\langle e_{1},\ldots,e_{i}^{\vee},\ldots,e_{n},v+e_{n+1}\rangle
which are ”visible from above” and which project exactly to the star subdivision of
\sigma
at at
v
. The lower boundary
\tau_{-}
is isomorphic to
\sigma
.

The above construction extends to any smooth toric variety
X=X_{\Sigma}
, and
v\in{\rm int}(\sigma)
,
\sigma\in\Sigma
. The fan
\Sigma_{B}
associated with
B
is the union of cones in
\Sigma
and those spanned by the vector
v+e_{n+1}
and the cones in the closed star
\overline{\operatorname{Star}(\tau,\Sigma)}
.

3.
Resolution of singularities in characteristic zero by weighted centers

3.1.
Resolution invariant on regular schemes with SNC divisors

3.1.1.
Compatibility with SNC divisors

Definition 3.1.2
.

A coordinate system (or a partial system of local parameters)
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
on an open subset
U
of a regular scheme
X

is compatible with
E
if the restriction
E_{i|U}
of any component
E_{i}
in
E
intersecting
U
is of the form
V(x_{i})
. If
p\in V(x_{i})
, where
V(x_{i})
determines a component in
E_{|U}
then we say that
x_{i}
is
divisorial
at
p
. Otherwise, we say that
x_{i}
is
free
at
p
.
We say that a Rees center
{\mathcal{A}}
is
is compatible with
E
at
p
or on
U
if it can be written as

{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[x_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{\operatorname{int}}\,\,\mbox{ or}\quad{\mathcal{A}}^{\operatorname{ext}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{-1/w},x_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]

where
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
is compatible with
E
at
p
or respectively on
U
.

Remark 3.1.3
.

This definition can be linked to the logarithmic language. The coordinate system defines at every point
p\in U
a regular map

U\to\operatorname{Spec}({\mathbb{Z}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}])=\operatorname{Spec}({\mathbb{Z}}[P]),

where the divisorial coordinates
x_{i}
at
p
generate a monoid
P=\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}_{p}
, and induce the chart
U\to\operatorname{Spec}({\mathbb{Z}}[P])
.
The free coordinates
x_{j}
determine a coordinate system on the stratum
s:=V(P\smallsetminus 0)
through
p
defined by the divisorial parameters.

3.1.4.
The centers compatible with an SNC divisor

Consider the total order on the set of symbols

{\mathbb{Q}}_{+}:={\mathbb{Q}}\,\amalg\,\{a_{+}\mid a\in{\mathbb{Q}}\},

by putting
a_{+}>a
, and
a_{+}<b
if
a<b
.
Similarly we define
{\mathbb{Z}}_{+}:={\mathbb{Z}}\cup\{a_{+}\mid a\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}
.
The following operations are defined on
{\mathbb{Q}}_{+}
:

•

addition

•

subtraction of the elements in
{\mathbb{Q}}
from an element in
{\mathbb{Q}}_{+}
.

•

multiplication of elements in
{\mathbb{Q}}_{+}
by the positive rational elements in
{\mathbb{Q}}_{>0}
.

Given a center
{\mathcal{A}}
compatible with
E
and its local presentation

{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[x_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{\operatorname{int}}

we associate with a divisorial coordinate
x_{i}\in E_{p}
the symbol
b_{i}:=a_{i+}
and with a free coordinate
x_{i}
simply
b_{i}:=a_{i}
.
We shall always assume that in the presentation:

b_{1}\leq\ldots\leq b_{k}.

For any such a center with the presentation
{\mathcal{A}}
compatible with
E
we can define

{\operatorname{inv}}({\mathcal{A}}):=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{k})

with
b_{1}\leq\ldots\leq b_{k}
.

Remark 3.1.5
.

Recall that the logarithmic order introduced in
[
ATW17
, Section 3.6]

associates with divisorial unknowns the infinity weight, and with free unknowns the weight
1
. This language is compatible with the logarithmic derivations and leads to the logarithmic resolution. Here we assign the weight
1_{+}
to the divisorial unknowns. This is the ”minimal weight” greater than the free unknowns weight, which is still equal to
1
.
The notion is compatible with the standard derivations and the standard transformation rules
and gives an SNC resolution. It prioritizes the divisorial unknowns without essentially changing their weights.

3.1.6.
Canonical invariant

In
[
ATW19
]
we introduced the resolution invariant used for the desingularization. The invariant is defined by the inductive procedure, and can be described in terms of
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideals. We shall modify this invariant below so it can be used in the SNC setting in the context of Rees algebra.

Consider the set

(({\mathbb{Q}}_{+})_{\geq 0})^{\leq n}\ \ :=\ \ \bigsqcup_{k\leq n}(({\mathbb{Q}}_{+})_{\geq 0})^{k}.

with lexicographic order. We compare the sequences of different lengths lexicographically by placing a sequence of
\infty
at the end.

Definition 3.1.7
.

Let
X
be a regular scheme with an SNC divisors
E
,
By
the
canonical invariant

{\operatorname{inv}}_{p}({\mathcal{I}})
of
{\mathcal{I}}
at a point
p
we shall mean

{\operatorname{inv}}_{p}({\mathcal{I}}):=\max\{(b_{1},\ldots,b_{k})\mid{\mathcal{I}}t\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,{x}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{{\operatorname{int}}}\mid(b_{1}\leq\ldots\leq b_{k})\}.

where
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})
are the compatible with
E
at
p
. Then
{\operatorname{inv}}_{p}({\mathcal{I}})\in({\mathbb{Q}}^{+}_{\geq 0})^{\leq n}\
if it exists.
The Rees center

{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,{x}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{{\operatorname{int}}}

such that
{\mathcal{I}}_{p}\subseteq{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,{x}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{{\operatorname{int}}}
and If
{\operatorname{inv}}_{p}({\mathcal{I}})={\operatorname{inv}}({\mathcal{A}})=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{k})
will be called
a maximal admissible center at
p
compatible with
E
.

Remark 3.1.8
.

It is not clear a priori, that
{\operatorname{inv}}_{p}({\mathcal{I}})
is well defined since the maximum may not be attained. It will be proven in Section
3.7.1
that there exists unique maximal admissible algebra
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{{\operatorname{int}}}
for which the maximum
(\overline{b}_{1},\ldots,\overline{b}_{k})
is attained.

The invariant
{\operatorname{inv}}_{p}({\mathcal{I}})
was introduced in

[
ATW19
]
in a different language in the case where no divisors are present giving the embedded desingularization without the divisorial SNC structure.

On the other hand
{\operatorname{inv}}_{p}({\mathcal{I}})
can be also associated with the part of the Hironaka resolution invariant developed in earlier papers on the resolution of singularities, in particular, Bierstone and Milman’s
[
BM97
]
, Villamayor’s

[
Vil89
]
, Encinas-Hauser
[
EH02
]
and Włodarczyk’s
[
Wło05
]
,
in the particular situation when no exceptional divisors are present, so at the beginning of the process.

3.1.9.
Presentation of centers

One can write centers in a more compact form:

{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{{\operatorname{int}}},

where
0<a_{1}<a_{2}\ldots<a_{k}
, and

\overline{x}_{1},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}
is a partial system of local parameters compatible with
E
on open
U
intersecting
V(\overline{x}_{1},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k})
and each
\overline{x}_{i}:=(x_{i1},\ldots,x_{ik_{i}})
is a subsystem of coordinates.

Then we associate with each block
\overline{x}_{i}t^{1/a_{i}}

the invariant
\overline{b_{i}}=(b_{i1},\ldots,b_{ik_{i}})
, where
b_{i1}\leq\ldots\leq b_{ik_{i}}
, such that
b_{ij}=a_{i}
if
x_{ij}
is free on
U
or
b_{ij}=a_{i+}
if
x_{ij}
is divisorial on
U
.
Consequently we can rewrite the definition of the resolution invariant in the form which is more convenient for computations and presentation.

{\operatorname{inv}}_{p}({\mathcal{I}})=\max\{(\overline{b}_{1},\ldots,\overline{b}_{k})\mid{\mathcal{I}}t\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{{\operatorname{int}}}\}

3.1.10.
Order of ideal revisited

One can see that the number
a_{1}
in the maximal admissible center is simply the order of ideal
a_{1}={\operatorname{ord}}_{p}({\mathcal{I}})
. In particular we have:

Lemma 3.1.11
.

{\operatorname{ord}}_{p}({\mathcal{I}})=\max\{a_{1}\in{\mathbb{Q}}_{>0}\mid\quad{\mathcal{I}}t\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[m_{p}t^{1/a_{1}}]\}

Proof.

We can write:

{\operatorname{ord}}_{p}({\mathcal{I}})=\max\{a_{1}\in{\mathbb{N}}\mid{\mathcal{I}}_{p}\subset m_{p}^{a_{1}}\}=\max\{a_{1}\in{\mathbb{Q}}_{>0}\mid{\mathcal{I}}_{p}\subset m_{p}^{\lceil a_{1}\rceil}\}

On the other hand the condition by Lemma
2.2.8
,

{\mathcal{I}}_{p}t\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[m_{p}t^{1/a_{1}}]
means that

{\mathcal{I}}_{p}\subset m_{p}^{\lceil a_{1}\rceil}
.
∎

3.1.12.
Admissibility condition for ideals

Lemma 3.1.13
.

The following conditions are equivalent in a neighborhood of
p\in X
for an ideal
{\mathcal{I}}
:

(1)

{\mathcal{I}}t\subset{\mathcal{A}}:={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[x_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{\operatorname{int}}

(2)

{\mathcal{I}}t\subset{\mathcal{A}}^{{\operatorname{ext}}}:={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{-1/w_{A}},x_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]

(3)

{\mathcal{I}}t^{w_{A}}\subset{\mathcal{A}}^{w_{A}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[x_{1}t^{w_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{w_{k}}]^{\operatorname{int}}
, where
w_{i}=w_{A}/a_{i}

(4)

{\mathcal{I}}t^{w_{A}}\subset({\mathcal{A}}^{\operatorname{ext}})^{w_{A}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{-1},x_{1}t^{w_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{w_{k}}]

Proof.

The condition (3) and (4) are obtained by rescaling the conditions in (1) and (2). On the other hand, by Lemma
2.1.11
,

({\mathcal{A}}^{{\operatorname{ext}}})_{\geq 0}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{-1},x_{1}t^{w_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{w_{k}}]_{\geq 0}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[x_{1}t^{w_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{w_{k}}]^{\operatorname{int}}={\mathcal{A}}.

This implies that condition (1) and (2) are equivalent.
∎

Definition 3.1.14
.

We say that the center
{\mathcal{A}}:={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[x_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{\operatorname{int}}
or
{\mathcal{A}}^{{\operatorname{ext}}}
is
admissible
for
{\mathcal{I}}
if one of the four equivalent conditions above holds.

3.1.15.
Admissibility of Rees algebras

We extend the admissibility definition to Rees algebras:

Definition 3.1.16
.

We say that the Rees center
{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[x_{1}t^{w_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{w_{k}}]^{\operatorname{int}}
is
admissible
for a Rees algebra
R
or simply
R
-admissible if one of the equivalent condition holds:

(1)

R\subseteq{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[x_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{\operatorname{Int}},

where the inclusion is considered with the integral closure ”Int” taken in the smallest ring
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{1/w_{R,A}}]
containing both algebras, or, equivalently in
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{1/w}]
for any multiple
w=n\cdot w_{R,A}
or for a sufficiently divisible
w
.

(2)

R\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{-1/w_{R,A}},x_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}].

(3)

R\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{-1/w},x_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}],
where
w=n\cdot w_{R,A}
.

Remark 3.1.17
.

Note that the inclusion is independent of choice of sufficiently divissible
w
.

3.1.18.
Resolution invariant of Rees algebras

We generalize Definition
3.1.7
to the Rees algebras:

Definition 3.1.19
.

By the
resolution invariant
of a Rees algebra
R
at a point
p
on a regular scheme
X
with an SNC divisor
E
we mean

{\operatorname{inv}}_{p}(R):=\max\{(\overline{b}_{1},\ldots,\overline{b}_{k})\mid R\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{{\operatorname{Int}}}\}

We show in Section
3.7.1
that there exists unique center
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{{\operatorname{int}}}
for which the maximum
(\overline{b}_{1},\ldots,\overline{b}_{k})
is attained.
We shall call it the
maximal admissible center
for
R
at
p
.

Remark 3.1.20
.

In the actual resolution process of the ideals all the occurring Rees algebras
R
satisfy
w_{R,A}=w_{A}
and thus the integral closure ”Int” and ”int” coincide.

One can also define a version of the resolution invariant used for the concept of maximal contact in Section
3.6.10
.

{\operatorname{inv}}_{p}^{1}(R):=\max\{\overline{b}_{1}\mid R\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{{\operatorname{Int}}}\}

In particular

Lemma 3.1.21
.

If
R\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{{\operatorname{Int}}}
(with
a_{1}<a_{2}\ldots<a_{k}
) then

{\operatorname{inv}}_{p}^{1}(R)\geq\overline{b}_{1}
.

3.1.22.
Order of Rees algebras

Consequently one generalizes the notion of the order of ideals to Rees algebras.

Definition 3.1.23
.

Let
X
be a regular scheme with and SNC divisor
E
.
By the
order
of the Rees algebra
R=\bigoplus_{a\in\Gamma}R_{a}t^{a}
at the point
p\in X
, we mean

{\operatorname{ord}}_{p}(R):=\min_{a\in\Gamma\smallsetminus 0}\{{\operatorname{ord}}_{p}(R_{a})/a\}

Since
R
is finitely generated
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-algebra the order is attained for a certain homogenous element
ft^{p}\in R_{a}t^{a}
, so that

{\operatorname{ord}}_{p}(R)={\operatorname{ord}}_{p}(ft^{a})={\operatorname{ord}}_{p}(f)/a.

This definition is a generalization of the order of an ideal:

{\operatorname{ord}}_{p}({\mathcal{I}})={\operatorname{ord}}_{p}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}[{\mathcal{I}}t]).

Moreover, if
R={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[{\mathcal{I}}t]
then
{\operatorname{ord}}_{p}({\mathcal{I}})={\operatorname{ord}}_{p}(R)
.

Lemma 3.1.24
.

If
R\subseteq{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{\operatorname{Int}}

then
a_{1}\leq{\operatorname{ord}}_{p}(R)
. In particular,

{\operatorname{ord}}_{p}(R)=\max\{a_{1}\in{\mathbb{Q}}\mid R\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[m_{p}t^{1/a_{1}}]^{\operatorname{Int}}\}.

Moreover, if
{\mathcal{A}}
is maximal admissible for
R
at
p
then
{\operatorname{ord}}_{p}(R)=a_{1}
.

We shall use the following

Lemma 3.1.25
.

{\operatorname{ord}}_{p}(R^{w})=w\cdot{\operatorname{ord}}_{p}(R)

∎

Proof.

(of Lemma
3.1.24
)
By the assumption,

R=\bigoplus R_{a}t^{a}\subseteq{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{\operatorname{int}}\subseteq{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[m_{p}t^{1/a_{1}}]^{\operatorname{Int}}.

Rescaling
t\mapsto t^{a_{1}}
gives
R^{a_{1}}=\bigoplus R_{a}t^{aa_{1}}\subseteq{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[m_{p}t]^{\operatorname{Int}}
. Thus
R_{a}\subseteq m_{p}^{\lceil aa_{1}\rceil}

whence, by definition, for any
a\in\Gamma_{R}
,

(1/a_{1}){\operatorname{ord}}_{p}(R_{a}t^{a})={\operatorname{ord}}_{p}(R_{a}t^{aa_{1}})\geq{\operatorname{ord}}_{p}(m_{p}^{\lceil aa_{1}\rceil}t^{aa_{1}})\geq 1,

and
{\operatorname{ord}}_{p}(R)\geq a_{1}
.

On the other hand, if
{\operatorname{ord}}_{p}(R)=a_{1}

then
for any
a\in\Gamma\smallsetminus\{0\}
we have
{\operatorname{ord}}_{p}(R_{a})\geq aa_{1}
, so
R_{a}\subseteq m_{p}^{\lceil aa_{1}\rceil}
or

R_{a}t^{a}\subseteq m^{\lceil aa_{1}\rceil}_{p}t^{a}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[m_{p}t^{1/a_{1}}]^{\operatorname{Int}}.

∎

3.1.26.
The invariants associated with Rees centers

Per analogy to the resolution invariant one can associate with the centers

{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{{\operatorname{int}}}

the invariant

{\operatorname{inv}}({\mathcal{A}})=(\overline{b}_{1},\ldots,\overline{b}_{k}),\quad{\operatorname{inv}}^{1}({\mathcal{A}}):=(\overline{b}_{1})

3.1.27.
Uniqueness of presentation of the invariant of centers

The following Lemma shows that
{\operatorname{inv}}({\mathcal{A}})
is well-defined and independent upon presentation.
We shall need the following result:

3.1.28.
Replacement Lemma

Lemma 3.1.29
.

[
ATW19
]
( non-divisorial case,
i=1
).

Let
{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots\overline{x}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{{\operatorname{int}}}
be a center and
p\in V({\mathcal{A}})
be a point.
Let
\overline{x}_{1},\ldots,\overline{x}_{i-1},\overline{x}^{\prime}
be a system of local parameters compatible with
E
at a point
p
, for some
i\leq k
, such that

\overline{x}^{\prime}t^{a_{i}}\in{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots\overline{x}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{\operatorname{int}}

in a neighborhood of
p
then
one can find the coordinates
\overline{x}_{1},\ldots,\overline{x}_{i-1},\overline{x}^{\prime}_{i},\overline{x}_{i+1}\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}
such that
\overline{x}\subset\overline{x}_{i}^{\prime}
and

{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x_{1}}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{i-1}t^{1/a_{i-1}},\overline{x}_{i}^{\prime}t^{1/a_{i}},\overline{x}_{i+1}t^{1/a_{i+1}}\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{\operatorname{int}}

in a neighborhood of
p\in X
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.2.8
,
{\mathcal{A}}_{a_{i}}\subset(\overline{x}_{1},\ldots,\overline{x}_{i})+m_{p}^{2}
.
Thus upon the coordinate change of
\overline{x}_{i}
compatible with
E
in image in
m_{p}/(m_{p}^{2}+(\overline{x}_{1},\ldots,\overline{x}_{i-1}))
, of the set of coordinates
\overline{x}
is a subset of the image of coordinates in
\overline{x}_{i}
. So one can extend
\overline{x}
, and assume that
\overline{x}
and
\overline{x}_{i}
define the same images in
m_{p}/(m_{p}^{2}+(\overline{x}_{1},\ldots,\overline{x}_{i-1}))
.
Then in the completion
\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{X,p}}
we can write
equality of the vectors of the coordinates

\overline{x}^{\prime}_{i}=\overline{x}_{i}+\overline{g},\quad\overline{x}^{\prime}_{j}=\overline{x}_{j},\quad j\neq i,

where the coordinates of vector
\overline{g}
are in
{\mathcal{A}}_{a_{1}}\cap(m_{p}^{2}+(\overline{x}_{1},\ldots,\overline{x}_{i-1}))
.
This determines an automorphism of
\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{X,p}}
which takes
\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{X,p}}\cdot{\mathcal{A}}
onto
\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{X,p}}\cdot{\mathcal{A}}
, and determines the desired coordinate change.
∎

Corollary 3.1.30
.

[
ATW19
]
Assume that a center
{\mathcal{A}}
has two different presentations:

{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots\overline{x}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{{\operatorname{int}}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x}^{\prime}_{1}t^{1/a^{\prime}_{1}},\ldots\overline{x}^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}t^{1/a_{k^{\prime}}}]^{{\operatorname{int}}}

then the associated invariants
(\overline{b}_{1},\ldots,\overline{b}_{k})=(\overline{b}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\overline{b}^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}})
are the same.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.1.24
,

a_{1}=a^{\prime}_{1}={\operatorname{ord}}_{p}({\mathcal{A}})
, which can be verifed for generators.
By Lemma
3.1.29
, applied to both presentations, we can assume that
\overline{x_{1}}=\overline{x^{\prime}_{1}}
.
Restricting both algebras to
V(\overline{x}_{1})=V(\overline{x^{\prime}}_{1})
we get the equality by the inductive assumption.
∎

3.2.
Motivating examples

3.2.1.
Simple isolated singularities in characteristic zero

Example 3.2.2
.

This is the main motivating example as it illustrates the invariant in simple terms. The example is generalized in Sections
4.2
, Example
4.2.4
and in Section
4.5
, Example
4.5.3
in any characteristic in two different ways.
Consider the isolated singularity defined at the origin
p
by

f=x_{1}^{c_{1}}+\ldots+x_{n}^{c_{n}},

on
X={\mathbb{A}}^{n}_{k}=\operatorname{Spec}k[x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}]
, where
c_{1}\leq\ldots\leq c_{n}
. Find the partition of
(c_{1},\ldots,c_{n})
:

c_{1}=\ldots=c_{i_{1}}<c_{i_{1}+1}=\ldots=c_{i_{2}}<\ldots<c_{i_{k-1}+1}=\ldots=c_{i_{k}}=c_{n},

and put
\overline{x}_{1}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i_{1}})
,
\overline{x}_{2}:=(x_{i_{1}+1},\ldots,x_{i_{2}})
,…,
\overline{x}_{k}=(x_{i_{k-1}+1},\ldots,x_{i_{k}})
,

{a}_{j}:=c_{i_{j-1}+1}=\ldots=c_{i_{j}}
.
By a slight abuse of notations we shall write:

f=\overline{x}_{1}^{{a}_{1}}+\ldots+\overline{x}_{k}^{{a}_{k}},

where

\overline{x}_{j}^{{a}_{j}}:=x_{i_{j-1}+1}^{{a}_{j}}+\ldots+x_{i_{j}}^{{{a}_{j}}}.

The goal is to find the maximal admissible Rees center
{\mathcal{A}}:={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x}^{\prime}_{1}t^{1/a^{\prime}_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}^{\prime}_{k}t^{1/a^{\prime}_{k}}]^{{\operatorname{int}}}
such that:

R:={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[(ft)]\subseteq{\mathcal{A}}:={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x}^{\prime}_{1}t^{1/a^{\prime}_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}^{\prime}_{k}t^{1/a^{\prime}_{k}}]^{{\operatorname{Int}}},

for a certain
k
and
a^{\prime}_{1}<a^{\prime}_{2}<\ldots<a^{\prime}_{k}
. The center
{\mathcal{A}}
will be constructed in the process of transformation of the algebra
R
and recursive adjoining the generators
\overline{x}^{\prime}_{1}t^{1/a^{\prime}_{1}}
.

We put
R_{1}=R
.
By Lemma
3.1.24
,

{\operatorname{ord}}_{p}(R_{1})={\operatorname{ord}}_{p}(ft)=a_{1}=a^{\prime}_{1}.

Applying iteratively the differential operators
t^{-1/a_{1}}{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
to the homogenous elements of positive degree of both sides of the admissibility inclusion
{\mathcal{R}}\subset{\mathcal{A}}
we see that left side
{\mathcal{A}}
will be preserved, while the right side
R_{1}
will be enlarged so that it contains so called
cotangent ideal

T^{1/a_{1}}({\mathcal{R}}_{1})\cdot t^{1/a_{1}}
that is the ideal
T^{1/a_{1}}({\mathcal{R}}_{1})
in gradation
t^{1/a_{1}}
of obtained from
R
by applying to
{\mathcal{I}}t
the iterated differential operators
t^{-1/a_{1}}{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
,
(a-1)
-times. More specifically:

T^{1/a_{1}}({\mathcal{R}}_{1})\cdot t^{1/a_{1}}:=(t^{-1}{\mathcal{D}}_{X})^{a_{1}-1}({\mathcal{I}}t)={\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{a_{1}-1}({\mathcal{I}})t^{1/a_{1}}\subseteq{\mathcal{A}}_{1/a_{1}}t^{1/a_{1}}\supseteq\overline{x}^{\prime}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}}.

Since
{\operatorname{ord}}_{p}({\mathcal{I}})=a_{1}
, we can see that
{\operatorname{ord}}_{p}(T^{1/a_{1}})={\operatorname{ord}}_{p}({\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{a_{1}-1}({\mathcal{I}}))=1
, and thus it contains a partial set of local parameters called

maximal contact
.
In our case

T^{1/a_{1}}(R_{1})={\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{a_{1}-1}(f)=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i_{1}},x_{i_{1}+1}^{a_{2}-a_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}^{a_{k}-a_{1}})

contains a maximal contact
\overline{x}_{1}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i_{1}})
.
In general,
by Lemma
3.6.20
, we can assume that using a simple transformation one can change the presentation of the algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
so that from now on
\overline{x}^{\prime}_{1}=\overline{x}_{1}
is a maximal contact of
R_{1}
.
This will not affect the algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
on the right just will change its presentation.

Next we construct
R_{2}
as the coefficient ideal

R_{2}:=C_{\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/{a_{1}}}}(R_{1})\subset{\mathcal{A}}

In our case we get

R_{2}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[(\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/{a_{1}}})^{\operatorname{Int}},ft]={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/{a_{1}}},ft].

Here the part
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\,\,\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}}]^{\operatorname{Int}}
, means the integral closure of
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\,\,\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}}]
in the smallest subalgebra
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{1/w}]={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{1/a_{1}}]
containing all the generators in
C_{\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}}}(R_{1})
.

In this example the splitting condition hold in the sense that we have inclusion

{\mathcal{O}}_{H_{1}}:={\mathcal{O}}_{X}/(\overline{x}_{1})=K[\overline{x}_{2},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}]\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X},

with
D_{\overline{x}_{1}}({\mathcal{O}}_{H_{1}})=0
. In general such a splitting is possible in the formal (or étale) coordinate system at a point
p\in X
. In such a case we can write

R_{2}=C_{\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/{a_{1}}}}(R_{1})={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[(\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/{a_{1}}})^{\operatorname{Int}},C_{\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/{a_{1}}}}(R_{1})_{|H_{1}}].

In fact the part
C_{\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/{a_{1}}}}(R_{1})_{|H_{1}}
is generated by the graded coefficients as in Lemma
3.5.6
.

To illustrate the construction in our case we can write

ft=(\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}})^{a_{1}}+(\overline{x}_{2}^{a_{2}}+\ldots+x_{k}^{a_{k}})t.

in the graded coefficient form with respect to
the graded coordinate
\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}}
. So the only graded coefficient is
f_{|V(x_{1})}\cdot t=(\overline{x}_{2}^{a_{2}}+\ldots+x_{n}^{a_{n}})t
. Consequently using presentation in Lemma
3.5.6
we simply write

R_{2}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[(\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}})^{\operatorname{Int}},(\overline{x}_{2}^{a_{2}}+\ldots+x_{n}^{a_{n}})t].

Moreover the maximal admissibility condition is satisfied with
R_{2}\subseteq{\mathcal{A}}
.

Moreover we say that
R_{2}
is
strictly nested
at
H_{1}=V(\overline{x}_{1})
, if it has the presentation

R_{2}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[(\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}})^{\operatorname{Int}},R_{2|H_{1}}],

where, in our case
R_{2|H_{1}}=(\overline{x}_{2}^{a_{2}}+\ldots+\overline{x}_{n}^{a_{n}})t
. (Definition
3.5.13
)

Then, by Lemma
3.5.11
,

{\mathcal{A}}_{|H_{1}}={\mathcal{O}}_{H_{1}}[\overline{x}^{\prime}_{2|H_{1}}t^{1/a^{\prime}_{2}},\ldots,\overline{x}^{\prime}_{k|H_{1}}t^{1/a^{\prime}_{k}}]^{{\operatorname{int}}}

is a maximal admissible center, for
R_{2|H_{1}}
and thus the restricted Rees algebra

R_{2|H_{1}}=(\overline{x}_{2}^{a_{2}}+\ldots+x_{n}^{a_{n}})t

is of order
a_{2}
. This implies that

a_{2}=a^{\prime}_{2}={\operatorname{ord}}_{p}(R_{2|H_{1}}).

We compute the maximal contact of
R_{2|H_{1}}=(\overline{x}_{2}^{a_{2}}+\ldots+\overline{x}_{n}^{a_{n}})t
to be the maximal subsystem of local parameters

\overline{x}_{2}\subset T^{1/a_{2}}(R_{2|H_{1}})={\mathcal{D}}_{\overline{x}_{2},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}}^{a_{2}-1}(f)={\mathcal{D}}_{\overline{x}_{2},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}}^{a_{2}-1}(\overline{x}_{2}^{a_{2}}+\ldots+\overline{x}_{k}^{a_{k}})\supseteq(\overline{x}_{2}).

Here
T^{1/a_{2}}(R_{2})
is obtained from
R_{2}
by applying the differential operators

t^{-(a_{2}-1)/a_{2}}{\mathcal{D}}_{\overline{x}_{2},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}}^{a_{2}-1}
to the generator
ft
of
R_{2}
in the gradation
t
, or in the split form to
ft_{|H_{1}}
.

Since
T^{1/a_{2}}(R_{2})\subset{\mathcal{A}}_{1/a}
we conclude that
\overline{x}^{\prime}_{2}=\overline{x}_{2}
, after a change of the coordinate presentation of
{\mathcal{A}}
.

Then, as before the coordinate
\overline{x}_{2}
splits and computing the coefficients of the only generator

f_{|H_{1}}=(\overline{x}_{2}^{a_{2}}+\ldots+\overline{x}_{n}^{a_{n}})t=(\overline{x}_{2}t^{1/a_{2}})^{a_{2}}+(\overline{x}_{3}^{a_{3}}+\ldots+\overline{x}_{n}^{a_{n}})t

of
R_{2|H_{1}}
gives the split form of the coefficient ideal as in Lemma
3.5.22
:

\displaystyle R_{3}:=C_{\overline{x}_{2}t^{1/{a_{1}}}}(R_{2})={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[(\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\overline{x}_{2}t^{1/a_{2}})^{\operatorname{Int}},f_{|H_{2}}]=

\displaystyle={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[(\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\overline{x}_{2}t^{1/a_{2}})^{\operatorname{Int}},(\overline{x}_{3}^{a_{3}}+\ldots+x_{n}^{a_{n}})t]\subset{\mathcal{A}},

where
H_{2}:=V(\overline{x}_{1},\overline{x}_{2})
. We maintain the maximal admissibility condition
R_{3}\subset{\mathcal{A}}
, while enlarging
R_{2}
.
At the same time
R_{3}
is in the strictly nested form for
\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\overline{x}_{2}t^{1/a_{2}}
(Definition
3.5.13
), which means exactly

R_{3}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}([\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\overline{x}_{2}t^{1/a_{2}})^{\operatorname{Int}},R_{3|H_{2}}]={\mathcal{O}}_{X}([\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\overline{x}_{2}t^{1/a_{2}})^{\operatorname{Int}},ft_{|H_{2}}]\subset{\mathcal{A}}

We continue the recursive process so that

R_{i+1}=C_{\overline{x}_{i}t^{1/{a_{i}}}}(R_{i})={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[(\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{i}t^{1/a_{1}})^{\operatorname{Int}},ft_{|H_{i}}]\subset{\mathcal{A}},

where
H_{i}=V(\overline{x}_{1},\ldots,\overline{x}_{i})
until
R_{k+1|H_{k}}=0
. The latter implies, after reverting to our original notation that

R_{k+1}={\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/{a}_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{\operatorname{Int}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[{x}_{1}t^{1/c_{1}},\ldots,{x}_{n}t^{1/c_{n}}]^{\operatorname{int}}.

Consequently,
{\operatorname{inv}}_{p}({\mathcal{I}})=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{n})
.
Since
{\mathcal{A}}
is an a priori chosen maximal
{\mathcal{I}}
-admissible center, which remains unchanged, we conclude that the process is independent of choices and thus canonical.

The full cobordant blow-up
B
of
X
at

{{\mathcal{A}}}^{{\operatorname{ext}}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{-1/w},\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{i}t^{1/a_{k}}],

where
w={\operatorname{lcm}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})
is defined by the rescaled algebra

{\mathcal{O}}_{B}=({{\mathcal{A}}}^{{\operatorname{ext}}})^{w}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{-1},\overline{x}_{1}t^{w_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}t^{w_{k}}],

where
w_{i}:=w/a_{i}
.

Equivalently one can write the center in the
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideal form
(\overline{x}_{1}^{1/w_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}^{1/w_{k}})
representing formally
t
-gradation of
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
.

Observe that

B=\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{-1},\overline{x}_{1}t^{w_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{i}t^{w_{k}}])=\operatorname{Spec}((K[t^{-1},\overline{x}_{1}t^{w_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{i}t^{w_{k}}])

is an affine space with coordinates
t^{-1}
and
x^{\prime}_{i}=x_{i}t^{w_{i}}
.

Now, the admissibility condition
ft\subset{{\mathcal{A}}}^{{\operatorname{ext}}}
implies that
ft^{w}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
. Here
ft^{w}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
is so called controlled transform
\sigma^{c}(f)
of
f
is obtained by factoring the inverse image by the power
(t^{-1})^{w}
, of the exceptional divisor
t^{-1}
.

Solving for
x_{i}
and substituting into
f
gives

f=t^{-w}((x^{\prime}_{1})^{a_{1}}+\ldots+(x^{\prime}_{n})^{a_{n}}).

After clearing the exceptional divisor we obtain the formula for

\sigma^{c}(f)=t^{a_{1}w_{1}}f=(x^{\prime}_{1})^{a_{1}}+\ldots+(x^{\prime}_{n})^{a_{n}}

in the new unknowns
x_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,x_{n}^{\prime}
on
B
which is identical to the original formula for

f
on
X
.
Thus the singularity locus on
B
at the vertex
V=V(x^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{n})
is described by the same formula, and
the cobordant blow-up
B_{+}=B\smallsetminus V(x^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{n})
is smooth so the resolution is obtained by the single cobordant blow-up.

3.2.3.
Generalizations

The previous example can be easily generalized without essential changes in computations.

Example 3.2.4
.

For the coordinate system
\overline{x}_{1},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}
, consider forms

F_{i}(\overline{x}_{i})
of degree
a_{i}
such that
a_{1}<\ldots<a_{k}
, and for which
(\overline{x}_{i})={\mathcal{D}}_{\overline{x}_{i}}^{a_{i}-1}(F_{i})
.
Then for
f=F_{1}+\ldots+F_{k}
and
{\mathcal{I}}=(f)
and
R=R_{1}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[ft]
,
As before we obtain the recursive formula

R_{i+1}=C_{\overline{x}_{i}t^{1/a_{i}}}(R_{i})={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[(\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{i}t^{1/a_{1}})^{\operatorname{Int}},f_{|H_{i}}\cdot t]\subset{\mathcal{A}},

where
H_{i}=V(\overline{x}_{1},\ldots,\overline{x}_{i})
,
f_{|H_{i}}=F_{i+1}+\ldots+F_{k}
and

\overline{x}_{i+1}t^{1/a_{i+1}}
is a maximal contact for
R_{i+1|H_{i}}={\mathcal{O}}_{H_{i}}[f_{|H_{i}}t]
.
In the inductive step we write

f_{|H_{i}}\cdot t=F_{i+1}(\overline{x}_{i+1}t^{1/a_{i+1}})+(F_{i+2}+\ldots+F_{k})t=F_{i+1}(\overline{x}_{i+1}t^{1/a_{i+1}})+f_{|H_{i+1}}\cdot t,

leading to the split formula for the
R_{i+2}=C_{\overline{x}_{i+1}t^{1/a_{i}}}(R_{i+1})
.
Consequently

R_{k+1}={\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[(\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/\overline{a}_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}})]^{\operatorname{Int}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,{x}_{n}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{\operatorname{int}}

is a maximal admissible center.

The full cobordant blow-up
B
of
X
at

{{\mathcal{A}}}^{{\operatorname{ext}}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{-1/w},\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{i}t^{1/a_{k}}],

where
w={\operatorname{lcm}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})
is defined by the rescaled algebra

{\mathcal{O}}_{B}=({{\mathcal{A}}}^{{\operatorname{ext}}})^{w}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{-1},\overline{x}_{1}t^{w_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}t^{w_{k}}],

where
w_{i}=w/a_{i}
.

It transforms
f
into its controlled transform

\sigma^{c}(f):=t^{w}f=F_{1}(\overline{x}^{\prime}_{1})+\ldots+F_{k}(\overline{x}^{\prime}_{k}),

For the new coordinates
\overline{x}^{\prime}_{i}=\sigma^{c}(\overline{x}^{\prime}_{i})=t^{w_{i}}\overline{x}_{i}
. Removing the vertex of the transfromation
we obtain
B_{+}=B\smallsetminus V(\overline{x}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\overline{x}^{\prime}_{k})
, where the order of one of the forms
F_{i}(\overline{x}^{\prime}_{i})
drops, which causes the invariant
{\operatorname{inv}}_{p}({\mathcal{I}})
drop.

In particular if

f=x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}+x_{4}^{4}+x_{5}^{2}x_{6}^{2}

then
R_{1}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[ft]
. The first (multiple) maximal contact is
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
for
a_{1}={\operatorname{ord}}_{p}(f)=3
.

Since
H_{1}=V((x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
splits we can write

R_{2}=C_{(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})t^{1/3}}(R_{1})={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})t^{1/3},(x_{4}^{4}+x_{5}^{2}x_{6}^{2})t]

for the graded coefficient decomposition for
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})t^{1/3}
:

ft=x_{1}t^{1/3}x_{2}t^{1/3}x_{3}t^{1/3}+(x_{4}^{4}+x_{5}^{2}x_{6}^{2})t

Then

a_{2}={\operatorname{ord}}_{p}(f_{|H_{1}})\cdot t={\operatorname{ord}}_{p}((x_{4}^{4}+x_{5}^{2}x_{6}^{2})t)=4/1=4

with maximal contact
(x_{4},x_{5},x_{6})t^{1/4}
, giving
the maximal admissible center

R_{3}=C_{(x_{4},x_{5},x_{6})t^{1/4}}(R_{2})={\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})t^{1/3},(x_{4},x_{5},x_{6})t^{1/4}]^{\operatorname{int}},

and the invariant
{\operatorname{inv}}_{p}(f)=(3,3,3,4,4,4)
. The extended center

{\mathcal{A}}^{\operatorname{ext}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{-1/12},(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})t^{1/3},(x_{4},x_{5},x_{6})t^{1/4}])

determines the full cobordant blow-up

B=\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{-1},(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})t^{4},(x_{4},x_{5},x_{6})t^{3}].

Alternatively one can write the center as the
{\mathbb{Q}}
-ideal

((x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})^{1/4},(x_{4},x_{5},x_{6})^{1/3})

formally representing
t
-gradation of
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
.

3.2.5.
Varieties with a divisorial SNC- structure

Example 3.2.6
.

Let
f=(x_{1}+x_{2}^{2})^{2}+x_{3}^{7}
, where
x_{1}
,
x_{2}
are divisorial at the origin at
x_{3}
is free. In this case the process is similar except finding maximal contact is slightly different as it depends on the divisorial structure.
Set
R=R_{1}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[ft]
. Then the order of
f
and of
R_{1}
at the origin is equal to
2
.
The computation of the cotangent ideal is the same
T^{1/2}(R)=D(f)=((x_{1}+x_{2}^{2}),x_{3}^{6})
.

The maximal contact for the Rees algebra of order
a_{1}
is given by the free coordinates in
T^{1/a_{1}}(R)
and the divisorial unknowns occurring in the linear parts of
T^{1/a_{1}}(R)
.
In our case the maximal contact
\overline{u}:=(x_{1})
for
R_{1}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[ft]
is determined by the only divisorial coordinate
x_{1}
tangential to
T^{1/2}(R)=((x_{1}+x_{2}^{2}),x_{3}^{6})
at
p
such that
\overline{u}+m_{p}^{2}\supset T^{1/2}(R)
. (Definitions
3.6.2
,
3.6.11
)

Write
ft=((x_{1}+x_{2}^{2})^{2}+x_{3}^{7})t
in the coefficient form

ft=((x_{1}+x_{2}^{2})^{2}+x_{3}^{7})t=(x_{1}t^{1/2})^{2}+(x_{1}t^{1/2})2x_{2}^{2}t^{1/2}+(x_{2}^{4}+x_{3}^{7})t.

with respect to the graded maximal contact
x_{1}t^{1/2}
.
We form the
coefficient ideal

R_{2}:=C_{x_{1}t^{1/2}}(R_{1})={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[(x_{1}t^{1/2})^{{\operatorname{Int}}},x_{2}^{2}t^{1/2},(x_{2}^{4}+x_{3}^{7})t].

Then
R_{2}
is in a strictly nested form:
R_{2}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[(x_{1}t^{1/2})^{{\operatorname{Int}}},R_{2|H_{1}}]
, where
H_{1}=V(x_{1})
.

The order
{\operatorname{ord}}_{p}(R_{2|H_{1}})=4
, with

T^{1/4}(R_{2})={\mathcal{D}}_{(x_{2},x_{3})}(x_{2}^{2})+{\mathcal{D}}^{3}_{(x_{2},x_{3})}((x_{2}^{4}+x_{3}^{7})=(x_{2},x_{3}^{4})

consisting of all elements in gradation

t^{1/4}
of the algebra obtained by applying iterated operators
{\mathcal{D}}_{(x_{2},x_{3})t^{1/4}}
to
R_{2}
.
Thus the maximal contact is equal to
x_{2}
in gradation
t^{1/4}
(Definition
3.6.2
).
Write
(x_{2}^{4}+x_{3}^{7})t\in R_{2}
in the coefficient form with respect to
x_{2}t^{1/4}
:

(x_{2}^{4}+x_{3}^{7})t=(x_{2}t^{1/4})^{2}+x_{3}^{7}t.

Thus

R_{3}:=C_{x_{2}t^{1/4}}(R_{3})={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[(x_{1}t^{1/2},x_{2}t^{1/4})^{\operatorname{Int}},x_{3}^{7}t].

The order of
R_{3|H_{2}}={\operatorname{ord}}_{0}(x_{3}^{7}t)=7
. The maximal contact is
x_{3}\in T^{7}(R_{3})={\mathcal{D}}^{6}_{x_{3}}(x_{3}^{7})
in gradation
t^{1/7}
.
Then

R_{4}:=C_{x_{3}t^{1/7}}(R_{3})={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[x_{1}t^{1/2},x_{2}t^{1/4},x_{3}t^{1/7}]^{{\operatorname{int}}}.

Since
R_{4|H_{3}}=0
, for
H_{3}=V(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
,we conclude that
{\mathcal{A}}=R_{4}
is a maximal admissible center for
(x_{1}+x_{2}^{2})^{2}+x_{3}^{7})
t .
Since
w={\operatorname{lcm}}(2,4,7)=28
we get the formula for the extended Rees center

{\mathcal{A}}^{\operatorname{ext}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{-1/28},x_{1}t^{1/2},x_{2}t^{1/4},x_{3}t^{1/7}]

Rescaling defines
the cobordant blow-up
B={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{-1},x_{1}t^{14},x_{2}t^{7},x_{3}t^{4}]
at

{\mathcal{A}}^{\operatorname{ext}}
. Since
x_{1},x_{2}
are divisorial and
x_{3}
is free, and
{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[x_{1}t^{1/2},x_{2}t^{1/4},x_{3}t^{1/7}]^{{\operatorname{int}}}
is a maximal admissible center one obtains

{\operatorname{inv}}_{0}(f)=(2_{+},4_{+},7).

The effect of the full cobordant blow-up is exactly

f=t^{-28}((x_{1}^{\prime}+(x_{2}^{\prime})^{2})^{2}+(x_{3}^{\prime})^{7}).

Let us examine this effect on the cobordant blow-up
B_{+}=B\smallsetminus V=B\smallsetminus V(x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2},x^{\prime}_{3})
after removing the vertex
V=V(x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2},x^{\prime}_{3})
.

On
B\smallsetminus V(x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2})
we write the equation
f^{\prime}:=t^{28}f
as
f^{\prime}=u^{2}+(x_{3}^{\prime})^{7}
, where

u:=x_{1}^{\prime}+(x_{2}^{\prime})^{2}

is a free coordinate. Thus
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}={\mathcal{O}}_{X^{\prime}}[u^{1/2},(x^{\prime}_{3})^{1/7}]^{\operatorname{int}}
is maximal admissible center for
(f^{\prime})
at
V(u,x^{\prime}_{3})
with the invariant

{\operatorname{inv}}_{p^{\prime}}(f^{\prime})=(2,7)<{\operatorname{inv}}_{0}(f)=(2_{+},4_{+},7)

for
p^{\prime}\in V(u,x^{\prime}_{3})
.

The next cobordant blow-up at
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}={\mathcal{O}}_{X^{\prime}}[ut^{1/2},(x^{\prime}_{1/3})t^{1/7}]^{\operatorname{int}}
resolves the singularity.

On
B\smallsetminus V(x_{3}^{\prime})
, the derivative

{\mathcal{D}}_{x^{\prime}_{3}}(f^{\prime})={\mathcal{D}}_{x^{\prime}_{3}}(x_{1}^{\prime}+(x_{2}^{\prime})^{2})^{2}+(x^{\prime}_{3})^{7})=(7x^{\prime}_{3})^{6}

is invertible and
f^{\prime}
is nonsingular.

In case there are no divisors on
X
, the process is faster as we can write immediately
f=u^{2}+x_{3}^{7}
, where
u=x_{1}+x_{2}^{2}
. So
{\operatorname{maxinv}}(f)=(2,7)
, and by the previous example,
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}={\mathcal{O}}_{X^{\prime}}[u^{t}{1/2},(x_{3}^{\prime})^{t}{1/7}]^{\operatorname{int}}
is the maximal admissible center. The cobordant blow-up of
{\mathcal{J}}=(u^{1/7},x_{3}^{1/2})
resolves the singularity.

3.3.
Splitting of derivations and compatibility

Splitting of derivations on regular schemes allow for the coefficient representations of functions and simplifies the computations of the coefficient ideals.
A similar concept in a different language was also used in
[
ATW19
]
.

Definition 3.3.1
.

We say that a closed subscheme
H
of a scheme
X

splits
if the closed embedding
i:H\hookrightarrow X
splits, so there is an affine morphism
\pi:X\to H
which is a left inverse of
i
with
\pi i=\text{id}_{H}
.

In other words for any open
U
, and any function
f\in{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(\pi^{-1}(U))
its
restriction

f_{|H}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{H}(U)\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(\pi^{-1}(U)).

The splitting condition implies that there is an injective morphism
\pi^{-1}({\mathcal{O}}_{H})\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
of sheaves on
X
with the left inverse
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\hookrightarrow i_{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{H})
given by the restriction.

The notion can be extended to derivations corresponding to a system of local parameters.

Definition 3.3.2
.

Consider a set of derivations
D_{\overline{x}}=(D_{x_{1}},\ldots,D_{x_{k}})
of
{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
on a regular scheme
X
for a certain partial system of coordinates
\overline{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})
such that
D_{x_{i}}(x_{j})=\delta_{ij}
,
we say that
D_{\overline{x}}

splits
in
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
, if
H=V(\overline{x})\subset X
splits and the derivations in
D_{\overline{x}}
vanish on
\pi^{-1}({\mathcal{O}}_{H})\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
.

Example 3.3.3
.

Let
X
be a variety smooth over a field
K
. Let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in m_{p}
be a complete coordinate system at a closed point
p
.
Any regular subscheme

H=V(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\subset\operatorname{Spec}(\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,p})=\operatorname{Spec}(K_{p}[[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]])

for any natural
k\leq n
, splits. Here
K_{p}={\mathcal{O}}_{X,x}/m_{p,X}
is the residue field at
p\in X
.
Indeed, we can always write

{\mathcal{O}}_{H,p}\simeq K_{p}[[x_{k+1},\ldots,x_{n}]]\subset K_{p}[[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]].

Moreover the system of derivations
D_{x_{1}},\ldots,D_{x_{k}}
on
\operatorname{Spec}(\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,p})
split as they vanish exactly on
K_{p}[[x_{k+1},\ldots,x_{n}]]
.

Both notions are strictly related:

Lemma 3.3.4
.

Let
H=V(\overline{x})
be a smooth subvariety on a smooth variety
X
.
If
H

splits on variety
X
, and
\pi:X\to H
is a splitting morphism, then there is a unique system of derivatives
D_{\overline{x}}
which splits in
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
, so that
D_{x_{i}}(x_{j})=\delta_{ij}
and
D_{\overline{x}}
vanishes on
\pi^{-1}({\mathcal{O}}_{H})
. Conversely, if
(H,D_{\overline{x}})
splits then
it defines a unique splitting morphism
\pi:X\to H
for the closed embedding
H
. Moreover
D_{\overline{x}}=\partial_{\overline{x}}
is computed by any coordinate coordinate system
(\overline{x},\overline{y})
, where
\overline{y}=\overline{y}_{|H}
.

Proof.

If
H
splits in
X
we extend
\overline{x}
to a complete system of coordinates
(\overline{x},\overline{y})
by adjoining a system of coordinates
\overline{y}\subset\pi^{-1}({\mathcal{O}}_{H})\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
. Then
D_{\overline{x}}=\partial_{\overline{x}}
is defined with respect to the coordinate system
(\overline{x},\overline{y})
. Conversely if
(H,D_{\overline{x}})
splits then
\pi^{-1}({\mathcal{O}}_{H})
is determined exactly by vanishing
D_{\overline{x}}
.
∎

Recall a well known fact in our setting:

Lemma 3.3.5
.

If
(\overline{x},D_{\overline{x}})
splits on a smooth variety
X
then for any open affine subset
U
, and
f\in{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(\pi^{-1}(U)
there is a decomposition of
f
up to
(x^{k})
for any
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

f\equiv\sum_{|\alpha|<k}c_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}(\mbox{mod}\,\,(x^{k})),

where

c_{\alpha}=\frac{1}{\alpha!}D_{x^{\alpha}}(f)_{|H}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{H}(U)\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(\pi^{-1}(U).

Proof.

Let
H=V(\overline{x})
.
Observe that
f-f_{|H}\in(\overline{x})
.

Note that

\frac{1}{\alpha!}D_{x^{\alpha}}(f-\sum_{|\alpha|<k}c_{\alpha}x^{\alpha})_{|H}=c_{\alpha}-c_{\alpha}\frac{1}{\alpha!}D_{x^{\alpha}}(x^{\alpha})=0.

Suppose that
g:=(f-\sum_{|\alpha|<k}c_{\alpha}x^{\alpha})\not\in\overline{x}^{k}
. Then there is a
l<k
, such that
g\in\overline{x}^{l}\smallsetminus\overline{x}^{l+1}
.
Then
g=\sum_{|\alpha|=l}b_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}
, where at least for one
\alpha_{0}
we have
b_{\alpha_{0}}\not\in(\overline{x})
.
Then

\frac{1}{\alpha_{0}!}D_{x^{\alpha_{0}}}(g)_{|H}=\sum(\frac{1}{\alpha_{0}!}D_{x^{\alpha_{0}}}b_{\alpha}x^{\alpha})_{|_{|H}}\neq 0,

which is a contradiction.
∎

Definition 3.3.6
.

Let
\overline{x}_{1}=(x_{11},\ldots,x_{1s_{1}})
be a partial system of coordinates on a a regular scheme
X
with
a set of derivations
D_{\overline{x}_{1}}:=(D_{x_{11}},\ldots,D_{x_{1s_{1}}})
such that
D_{x_{1j}}(x_{1i})=\delta_{ij}
. We say that a system of derivations
D_{\overline{x}_{1}}
is
compatible with a Rees center
{\mathcal{A}}
on
X
if there is a certain presentation

A={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x_{k}}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{\operatorname{int}},

such that
so that
D_{\overline{x}_{1}}(\overline{x}_{i})=0
for
i\geq 2
.

Lemma 3.3.7
.

Let
A={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x_{k}}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{\operatorname{int}}
be a center.
If
(\overline{x},D_{\overline{x}_{1}})
splits in
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
then it is compatible with the center
{\mathcal{A}}
.

Proof.

Set
\overline{x}^{\prime}_{i}:=\overline{x}_{i|H_{1}}
. Then
\overline{x}^{\prime}_{i}-\overline{x}_{i}\in(\overline{x}_{1})
, and since
a_{i}>a_{1}
, we have that
\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{i}}\in{\mathcal{A}}
. So

\overline{x}^{\prime}_{i}t^{1/a_{i}}-\overline{x}_{i}t^{1/a_{i}}\in(\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{i}})\subset{\mathcal{A}}.

Hence
\overline{x}^{\prime}_{i}t^{1/a_{i}}\in{\mathcal{A}}
and we have inclusion of
the centers
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x_{k}}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{\operatorname{int}}\subseteq{\mathcal{A}}
. By symmetry we have the reverse inclusion. ∎

Corollary 3.3.8
.

If
X
is smooth over a field
K
then any derivation
D_{\overline{x}_{1}}
splits in
\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{X,p}}
so it is compatible with any center of the form

A=\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{X,p}}[\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{\operatorname{int}}.

∎

3.4.
Derivations on the Rees centers

For an ideal
{\mathcal{I}}
of order
a\in{\mathbb{N}}
at a point
p\in X
on a smooth variety
X
we can place it in
t^{a}
-gradation of a certain
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded Rees algebra
R={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[{\mathcal{I}}t^{a}]
so that we have the admissibility condition

R={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[{\mathcal{I}}t^{a}]\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[m_{p}t],

with respect to maximal ideal
m_{p}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X,p}
. Here we naturally associate with the coordinates gradation
t
, and since the derivatives
D_{x_{i}}
lower the order of ideals by
1
and we associate with with gradation
t^{-1}
. Consequently the graded derivations
t^{-1}D_{x_{i}}
act on the elements of
R\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t]
with positive gradations.
Moreover they preserve
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[m_{p}t]
.

In our approach we place the ideal
{\mathcal{I}}
in gradation
t
. Then the order of the Rees algebra
R={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[{\mathcal{I}}t]
at a point
p
is
a_{1}={\operatorname{ord}}_{p}({\mathcal{I}})
and thus the corresponding coordinates and derivations shall be rescaled accordingly and we consider the action of
t^{-1/a_{1}}D_{x_{i}}
. A similar concept was used in the first version of
[
ATW19
]
to interpret the algebra of the center.

In general, let
R=\bigoplus R_{a}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{1/w}]
be any Rees algebra on a smooth
X
.
For any local coordinate system
\overline{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
compatible with
E
on a smooth variety
X
, and a given
a_{1}\in{\mathbb{Q}}
(which is usually the order of the Rees algebra
R
) one considers graded derivations

D_{x_{i}t^{1/a_{1}}}:=t^{-1/a_{1}}D_{x_{i}}

acting on the elements of
R_{a}
of the gradations
a\geq 1/a_{1}
.

Consequently, if
ft^{a}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{1/w}]
, where
a\geq 1/a_{1}
then

D_{x_{i}t^{1/a_{1}}}(ft^{a})=D_{x_{i}}(f)t^{a-1/a_{1}}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{1/w}].

The idea is the following if

R\subseteq{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x_{k}}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{{\operatorname{Int}}}

then when applying the differential operators
D_{\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}}}
to the both sides we preserve the right side while enlarging the left side by adding new elements.
This way we step by step enlarge
R
which at some point of the algorithm becomes equal to the center
{\mathcal{A}}
on the right side.

More generally, by composing
D_{\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}}}
we consider differential operators:

D_{\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}}}^{\alpha}:=t^{-|\alpha|/a_{i}}D_{\overline{x}^{\alpha}},\quad\mbox{where}

D_{\overline{x}^{\alpha}}:=\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}!\ldots\alpha_{k}!}\cdot\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|}}{\partial x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\ldots\partial x_{n}^{\alpha_{n}}},\quad|\alpha|=\alpha_{1}+\ldots+\alpha_{n}.

acting on elements
ft^{a}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{1/w}]
and
a\geq|\alpha|/a_{1}
:

D_{\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}}}^{\alpha}ft^{a}=D_{\overline{x}^{\alpha}}(f)t^{a-|\alpha|/a_{1}}

Example 3.4.1
.

If
ft=(x^{3}+x^{2}y+y^{3})t
, and
a_{1}=3
then

D_{xt^{1/3}}((x^{3}+x^{2}y+y^{3})t)=(3x^{2}+2xy)t^{2/3}.

3.4.2.
Differential operators preserving centers

A main consequence of the condition
a_{1}\leq\ldots\leq a_{n}
is that the center
A
is preserved by the action of
D_{x_{i}t^{1/a_{1}}}
for
i=1,\ldots,k
.

Lemma 3.4.3
.

Let
A={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,{x_{k}}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{\operatorname{int}}
, and
\overline{x}=({x}_{1},\ldots,{x}_{n})
, where
n\geq k
, be a complete coordinate system on
X
extending
({x}_{1},\ldots,{x}_{k})
.
If
ft^{a}\in A_{a}t^{a}
, and
|\alpha|/a_{1}<a
then

D_{\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}}}^{\alpha}(ft^{a})\in A_{a-(|\alpha|/a_{1})}t^{a-(|\alpha|/a_{1})},

Proof.

The property can be verified on monomials
(x_{1}^{b_{1}}\cdot\ldots\cdot x_{k}^{b_{k}})t^{a}\in A_{a}t^{a}
and on the differential operators
D_{x_{i}t^{1/a_{1}}}
for
i=1,\ldots,k
.
If
x_{1}^{b_{1}}\cdot\ldots\cdot x_{k}^{b_{k}}\in A_{a}
then

b_{1}/a_{1}+\ldots b_{k}/a_{k}\geq a
.
So

b_{1}/a_{1}+\ldots+(b_{i}-1)/a_{i}+\ldots+b_{k}/a_{k}\geq a-(1/a_{1})

and

D_{x_{i}t^{1/a_{1}}}(x_{1}^{b_{1}}\cdot\ldots\cdot x_{k}^{b_{k}}\cdot t^{a})\sim(x_{1}^{b_{1}}\cdot\ldots\cdot x_{i}^{b_{i}-1}\cdot\ldots\cdot x_{k}^{b_{k}}\cdot t^{a-(1/a_{1})})\in A_{a-(1/a_{1})}

∎

3.5.
Coefficient ideal of Rees algebra

The origin of the coefficient ideals concept can be traced back to the work of Abhyankhar and Hironaka, as seen in
[
Hir64
]
. Various definitions of this notion have been explored in multiple studies, including
[
Vil89
]
,
[
BM97
]
,
[
Wło05
]
, and
[
Kol07
]
, among others.

In the context of our methodology, the coefficient ideal is used to derive a better approximation of a maximal admissible center. As demonstrated in Section
3.7.1
, the maximal admissible center
{\mathcal{A}}
can be obtained by the recursive application of coefficient ideals to a specified Rees algebra
R={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[{\mathcal{I}}t]
. The coefficient ideal, as per our definition, is a non-canonical concept that is relatively straightforward to compute in the completion
\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,p}
in what we refer to as a ’split form’ (as discussed in Section
3.5.4
).

This version of the coefficient ideal simplifies and streamlines computations and can be likened to the approach used by Bierstone-Milman in their resolution algorithm, as referenced in
[
BM91
]
and
[
BM97
]
, albeit in a differing framework.

Remark 3.5.1
.

If
D_{\overline{x}}
splits in
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
then
any
f\in{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
can be written in the coefficient form

f=\sum c_{\alpha}\overline{x}^{\alpha},

where
c_{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{V({\overline{x}})}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
so
we can obtain a particularly nice description of the center on
X
. This can be always done in the completion
\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,p}
.

Definition 3.5.2
.

Let
a_{1}\in{\mathbb{Q}}_{>0}
, and
R={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[f_{j}t^{b_{j}}]_{j=1,\ldots,s}
be a Rees algebra generated by
f_{j}t^{b_{j}}
for
j=1,\ldots,s
and let
\overline{x}
be any partial system of local coordinates compatible with
E
at
p
, and set
H:=V(\overline{x})
. By the
coefficient ideal with respect to
\overline{x}t^{{1/a_{1}}}
we mean the Rees algebra:

C_{\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}}}(R):={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[(\,\,\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}})^{\operatorname{Int}},D_{\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}}}^{\alpha}(f_{j}t^{b_{j}}),\quad|\alpha|<b_{j}a_{1},\,\,j=1,\ldots,s\,]

Recall that the part
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\,\,\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}}]^{\operatorname{Int}}
, means the integral closure of
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\,\,\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}}]
in the smallest subalgebra
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[t^{1/w}]
containing all the generators in
C_{\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}}}(R)
. Thus

{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\,\,\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}}]^{\operatorname{Int}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x}t^{1/w},\ldots,\overline{x}t^{w_{1}/w}],

where
w_{1}=w/a_{1}
, so
\overline{x}t^{w_{1}/w}=\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}}
.

Remark 3.5.3
.

The key concept of the coefficient ideal as well as the role of the graded local parameters
\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}}
are encapsulated by Lemma
3.5.11
. The coefficient ideal maintains admissibility property, while containing already some generators
\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}}
from a to be constructed maximal admissible center
{\mathcal{A}}
. Moreover after splitting it is generated by
(\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}})^{\operatorname{Int}}
and the part
C_{\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}}}(R)_{|V(\overline{x}_{1}}
which is independent of the coordinates
\overline{x}_{1}
.

3.5.4.
Coefficient ideal in the split form

The process of computing the coefficient ideal becomes remarkably straightforward when utilizing splitting derivations
D_{\overline{x}}
in
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
. This can be always done by passing to the completion
\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,p}
or to a corresponding étale neighborhood.
Assume that
(\overline{x},D_{\overline{x}})
splits on
X
.
For any element
f_{j}t^{b_{j}}\in R_{b_{j}}
, one can write
f_{j}
in
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}
we can write

f_{j}\equiv\sum c_{j\alpha}\overline{x}^{\alpha}=\sum_{|\alpha|<b_{j}a_{1}}c_{j\alpha}\overline{x}^{\alpha}+\sum_{|\alpha|\geq b_{j}a_{1}}c_{j\alpha}\overline{x}^{\alpha}\in\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X},p

where
c_{j\alpha}\in\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{H}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
for
H:=V(\overline{x})
.
Then, for
|\alpha|<b_{j}a_{1}

we have

D^{\alpha}_{\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}}}(f_{j}t^{b_{j}})_{|H}=c_{j\alpha}t^{b_{j}-|\alpha|/a_{1}}

Thus the restricted coefficient ideal can be represented in the format which is simpler for computations and justifies the name ”coefficient”:

Lemma 3.5.5
.

C_{\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}}}(R)_{|H}:={\mathcal{O}}_{H}[\,D_{\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}}}^{\alpha}(f_{j}t^{b_{j}})_{|H},|\alpha|<b_{j}a_{1}]={\mathcal{O}}_{H}[\,c_{j\alpha}t^{b_{j}-|\alpha|/a_{1}},|\alpha|<b_{j}a_{1}\,]

As a corollary from the above we obtain:

Lemma 3.5.6
.

Suppose
D_{\overline{x}}
splits in
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
. Then, with the above notations and the assumptions the coefficient ideal
C_{\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}}}(R)
can be written in the split form:

\displaystyle C_{\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}}}(R)={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[(\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}})^{\operatorname{Int}},C_{\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}}}(R)_{|H}]=

\displaystyle={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[(\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}})^{\operatorname{Int}},\,c_{j\alpha}t^{b_{j}-|\alpha|/a_{1}},|\alpha|<b_{j}a_{1}\,]

where
c_{j\alpha}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{H}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
, for
H=V(\overline{x})
.

Proof.

Let
R={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[f_{j}t^{b_{j}}]
. In the presentation

f_{j}=\sum c_{j\alpha}\overline{x}^{\alpha}=\sum_{|\alpha|<aa_{1}}c_{j\alpha}\overline{x}^{\alpha}+\sum_{|\alpha|\geq aa_{1}}c_{j\alpha}\overline{x}^{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{X}

the term

(\sum_{|\alpha|\geq b_{j}a_{1}}c_{j\alpha}\overline{x}^{\alpha})t^{b_{j}}=(f_{j}-\sum_{|\alpha|<b_{j}a_{1}}c_{j\alpha}\overline{x}^{\alpha})t^{b_{j}}\in\,\,{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}}]^{\operatorname{Int}}

Then we have

f^{0}_{j}t^{b_{j}}:=\sum_{|\alpha|<b_{j}a_{1}}c_{j\alpha}\overline{x}^{\alpha}t^{b_{j}}\,\,\in\,\,C_{\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}}}(R)

Thus for any maximal
\alpha_{j}^{0}
in the above presentation we have

\frac{1}{\alpha_{0}!}D^{\alpha_{j}^{0}}_{\overline{x}t^{1/a}}(f^{0}_{j}t^{b_{j}})=c_{j\alpha_{j}^{0}}t^{b_{j}-|\alpha_{j}^{0}|/a_{1}}\in C_{\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}}}(R).

Thus

c_{j\alpha_{j}^{0}}x^{\alpha_{0}}t^{b_{j}}=c_{j\alpha_{j}^{0}}t^{b_{j}-|\alpha_{j}^{0}|/a_{1}}\cdot x^{\alpha_{j}^{0}}t^{|\alpha_{j}^{0}|/a_{1}}\in C_{\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}}}(R).

Next consider

f^{1}_{j}t^{b_{j}}:=f^{0}_{j}t^{b_{j}}-c_{j\alpha_{j}^{0}}x^{\alpha_{0}}t^{b_{j}}\in C_{\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}}}(R),

and repeat the process for maximal
\alpha_{j}^{1}
in
f^{1}_{j}
, and continue inductively for
\alpha_{j}^{i}
in
f^{i}_{j}
until
f^{k}_{j}=0
.
∎

In particular, we have

Corollary 3.5.7
.

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{X,p}}\cdot{\mathcal{C}}_{\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}}}(R)=\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{X,p}}[(\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}})^{\operatorname{Int}},{\mathcal{C}}_{\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}}}(R)_{|H}]=

\displaystyle=\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,p}[(\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}})^{\operatorname{Int}},\,c_{j\alpha}t^{b_{j}-|\alpha|/a_{1}},|\alpha|<b_{j}a_{1}\,].

3.5.8.
Split vs non-split form of the coefficient ideal

The split form of the coefficient ideal is highly efficient for computations.
The subsequent example provides a comparison between two methods used for computing the coefficient ideal:

Example 3.5.9
.

Let
a_{1}=3
then
H=V(x)
splits in
X=\operatorname{Spec}(K[x,y,z]
and

ft=(x^{3}+x^{2}yw+z^{n})t=(xt^{1/3})^{3}+(xt^{1/3})^{2}ywt^{1/3}+z^{n}t

has coefficients
ywt^{1/3}
, and
z^{n}t
. Thus the split form of the coefficient ideal :

C_{xt^{1/3}}(x^{3}+x^{2}yw+z^{n})t={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[(xt^{1/3})^{\operatorname{Int}},ywt^{1/3},z^{n}t]

is generated by
(xt^{1/3})^{\operatorname{Int}}
and the coefficients

ywt^{2/3},z^{n}t
.

Note that the standard form of the coefficient ideal is more complicated, though still easily computable in this case:

\displaystyle C_{xt^{1/3}}(x^{3}+x^{2}yw+z^{n})t={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[(xt^{1/3})^{\operatorname{Int}},ft,D_{xt^{1/3}}(ft),D^{2}_{xt^{1/3}}(ft)]=

\displaystyle={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[(xt^{1/3})^{\operatorname{Int}},(x^{3}+x^{2}yw+z^{n})t,(3x^{2}+2xyw)t^{2/3},ywt^{1/3}],

3.5.10.
Coefficient ideals and admissibility

Lemma 3.5.11
.

Let
R={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[f_{j}t^{b_{j}}]
be the Rees algebra on
X
. The following conditions are equivalent in a neighborhood of
p\in X
.

(1)

R\subseteq{\mathcal{A}}^{\operatorname{Int}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x_{k}}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{{\operatorname{Int}}}
.

(2)

{\mathcal{C}}_{\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}}}(R)\subseteq{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x_{k}}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{\operatorname{Int}}

(3)

{\mathcal{C}}_{\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}}}((R)_{|H}\subseteq{\mathcal{A}}_{|H}={\mathcal{O}}_{H}[\overline{x}_{2|H}t^{1/a_{2}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k|H}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{\operatorname{Int}}
.

Proof.

We can pass to the completion
\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,p}
, and replace
\overline{x}_{i}
with
\overline{x_{i|H}}
, so that
D_{\overline{x}_{1}}
split and are compatible with
{\mathcal{A}}
.
Then
{\mathcal{C}}_{\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}}}(R)
is obtained from
R
by applying
{\mathcal{D}}_{\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}}}
. Note that
D_{\overline{x}_{1}}
splits, it is compatible with
\overline{x}_{i|H}
in
\overline{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,p}
.

(1)\Rightarrow(2)
by Lemma
3.4.3
, the operator
{\mathcal{D}}_{\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}}}
preserves both sides of the inclusion in (1) giving (2).

(2)\Rightarrow(3)
The assertion in (3) follows from (2) simply by taking the restriction.

(3)\Rightarrow(1)
By Lemma
3.5.6
,
{\mathcal{C}}_{\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}}}(R)\supset R
can be written in the split form

{\mathcal{C}}_{\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}}}(R)={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[(\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}})^{\operatorname{Int}},{\mathcal{C}}_{\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}}}((R)_{|H}]\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x_{k}}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{\operatorname{Int}}.

∎

3.5.12.
Nested Rees algebras

The following definition is motivated by the construction of the coefficient ideal.

Definition 3.5.13
.

Given a partial system
\overline{x}_{1},\ldots,\overline{x}_{n}
of local parameters compatible with
E
. Let

R_{|H_{k}}=\bigoplus R_{a|H_{k}}t^{a}

be the restriction of
R
to
H_{k}:=V(\overline{x}_{1},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k})
, for
k\leq n
, and let

0<a_{1}<a_{2}\ldots<a_{k}

be a sequence of rational numbers.

We say that the Rees algebra
R
is
strictly nested at
H_{k}

if
H_{k}\subset X
splits and

R={{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}}[(\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}})^{\operatorname{Int}},R_{|H_{k}}].

We say that the Rees algebra
R
is

nested at
H_{k}
if it contains subalgebra

{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[x_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{\operatorname{Int}}

and such that

\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,p}\cdot R
is strictly nested at
H_{k}
:

{\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{X,p}}}\cdot R={\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{X,p}}}[(\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}})^{\operatorname{Int}},R_{|H_{k}}],

The order
{\operatorname{ord}}_{p}(R_{|H_{k}})
will be called the
nested order
of
R
at
H_{k}
and
p
.

Using this terminology one can rephrase the Lemma
3.5.6
we get

Lemma 3.5.14
.

The coefficient ideal
C_{\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}}}(R)

is nested at
H=V(\overline{x})
.

Moreover, if
(H,D_{\overline{x}})
splits in
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}
then
C_{\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}}}(R)
is strictly nested at
H
:

C_{\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}}}(R)={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[(\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}})^{\operatorname{Int}},C_{\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}}}(R)_{|H}]

∎

In particular we can always write

\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,p}\cdot C_{\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}}}(R)=\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,p}[(\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}})^{\operatorname{Int}},C_{\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}}}(R)_{|H}]

3.5.15.
Nested coefficient ideals

The definition of the coefficient ideal can be extended to the setting of nested Rees algebras.

Definition 3.5.16
.

Let
\overline{x}_{1},\ldots,\overline{x}_{n}
be a local system of local coordinates.
Let

R={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[(\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}})^{\operatorname{Int}},f_{j}t^{b_{j}}]_{j=1,\ldots,s}

be the Rees algebra nested at
H_{k}=V(\overline{x}_{1},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k})
. Assume
a_{k+1}\geq a_{k}\geq\ldots\geq a_{1}
.
Then we shall call the coefficient ideal
C_{\overline{x}_{k+1}t^{1/a_{k+1}}}(R)
at
\overline{x}_{k+1}t^{1/a_{k+1}}

nested
for
R
at
H_{k}=V(\overline{x}_{1},\ldots,\overline{x}_{1})
.

Lemma 3.5.17
.

With the above notation the nested coefficient ideal of
R
at
\overline{x}_{k+1}t^{1/a_{k+1}},
with respect to
H_{k}
can be written as

\displaystyle C_{\overline{x}_{k+1}t^{1/a_{k+1}}}(R)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[(x_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,x_{k+1}t^{1/a_{k+1}})^{\operatorname{Int}},D_{\overline{x}_{k+1}t^{1/a_{k+1}}}^{\alpha}(f_{j}t^{b_{j}}),|\alpha|/a_{k+1}\leq b_{j}]

The subsequent statement is a straightforward extension of Lemma
3.5.7
:

Lemma 3.5.18
.

Let
R
be a Rees algebra nested at
H_{k}=V(\overline{x}_{1},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k+1})
. The nested coefficient ideal
C_{\overline{x}_{k+1}t^{1/a_{k+1}}}(R)
at
\overline{x}_{k+1}t^{1/a_{k+1}}
is a nested Rees algebra at
H_{k+1}=V(\overline{x}_{1},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k+1})
.

Proof.

By the assumption

\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,p}\cdot R=\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,p}[(\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}})^{\operatorname{Int}},R_{|H_{k}}]

Then by the above

\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,p}\cdot C_{\overline{x}_{k+1}t^{1/a_{k+1}}}(R)=\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,p}[(\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}})^{\operatorname{Int}},C_{\overline{x}_{k+1}t^{1/a_{k+1}}}(R_{|H_{k}})]

is also nested in
H_{k}
.
On the other hand,
by Lemma
3.5.7
,
C_{\overline{x}_{k+1}t^{1/a_{k+1}}}(R_{|H_{k}})
is nested in
H_{k+1}
on
H_{k}
. Consequently
C_{\overline{x}_{k+1}t^{1/a_{k+1}}}(R)
is nested on
X
at
H_{k+1}
. ∎

3.5.19.
Nested coefficient ideals in the split form

We can adapt Lemma
3.5.6
to strictly nested Rees algebras:
Let
\overline{x}_{1},\ldots,\overline{x}_{n}
be a system of local coordinates compatible with
E
at
p
.
Let

\displaystyle R={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[(\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}})^{\operatorname{Int}},f_{j}t^{b_{j}}]_{j=1,\ldots,s}

be a Rees algebra nested at
H_{k}=V(\overline{x}_{1},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k})
, for
k+1\leq n
.
Then

\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,p}\cdot R=\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,p}[(\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}})^{\operatorname{Int}},f_{j|H_{k}}t^{b_{j}}]_{j=1,\ldots,s}

with
f_{j|H_{k}}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{H_{k}}
.
Moreover
({x}_{k+1},D_{\overline{x}_{k+1}})
splits in
\operatorname{Spec}(\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,p})
and
we can write each generator
f_{j|H_{k}}t^{b_{j}}
as

\displaystyle f_{j|H_{k}}t^{b_{j}}\equiv\sum_{|\alpha|<aa_{k+1}}c_{j\alpha}\overline{x}_{k+1}^{\alpha}t^{b_{j}}\equiv\sum_{|\alpha|<aa_{k+1}}(c_{j\alpha}t^{b_{j}-|\alpha|/a_{k+1}})\overline{x}_{k+1}^{\alpha}t^{|\alpha|/a_{k+1}}

modulo the ideal

\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,p}((\overline{x}_{k+1})^{\alpha}t^{b_{j}}\mid|\alpha|\geq b_{j}a_{k+1})\subset\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,p}[\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{\operatorname{Int}},

where
c_{j\alpha}=c_{j\alpha}(\overline{x}_{k+2},\ldots,\overline{x}_{n})\in{\mathcal{O}}_{H_{k+1}}\subset\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,p}
.
Thus we obtain the result:

Lemma 3.5.20
.

With the above assumptions and notations the completion of the nested coefficient ideal with respect to
\overline{x}_{k+1}t^{1/a_{k+1}}
is equal to:

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,p}\cdot C_{\overline{x}_{k+1}t^{1/a_{k+1}}}(R)=\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,p}[(\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}})^{\operatorname{Int}},C_{\overline{x}_{k+1}t^{1/a_{k+1}}}(R)_{|H}]=

\displaystyle=\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,p}[(\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k+1}t^{1/a_{k+1}})^{\operatorname{Int}},\,c_{j\alpha}t^{b_{j}-|\alpha|/a_{k+1}},|\alpha|<b_{j}a_{k+1}\,]

with
c_{j\alpha}=c_{j\alpha}(\overline{x}_{k+2},\ldots,\overline{x}_{n})\in{\mathcal{O}}_{H_{k+1}}\subset\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,p}
.

∎

3.5.21.
Strictly nested coefficient ideals in the split form

The result can be stated for the strictly nested Rees algebras in view of Lemma
3.3.4
.

Lemma 3.5.22
.

Let
\overline{x}_{1},\ldots,\overline{x}_{n}
be a complete coordinate system. Let

\displaystyle R={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[(\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}})^{\operatorname{Int}},f_{j}t^{b_{j}}]_{j=1,\ldots,s}

be a Rees algebra nested at
H_{k}=V(\overline{x}_{1},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k})
, for
k\leq n-1
, where
f_{j}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{H_{k}}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
.
Assume that
H_{k+1}\subset X
splits. Then upon the coordinate change
\overline{x}_{1},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k},\overline{x}_{k+1|H_{k}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{n|H_{k}}
,

D_{\overline{x}_{k+1}}=\partial_{\overline{x}_{k+1}}
splits in
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
and the coefficient ideal
C_{\overline{x}_{k+1}t^{1/a_{k+1}}}(R)
is strictly nested at
H_{k+1}
and admits the split form

C_{\overline{x}_{k+1}t^{1/a_{k+1}}}(R)={{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}[(\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k+1}t^{1/a_{k+1}})^{\operatorname{Int}},\,c_{j\alpha}t^{b_{j}-|\alpha|/a_{k+1}},|\alpha|<b_{j}a_{k+1}\,]

with
c_{j\alpha}=c_{j\alpha}(\overline{x}_{k+2},\ldots,\overline{x}_{n})\in{\mathcal{O}}_{H_{k+1}}\subset\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}
. ∎

Example 3.5.23
.

Let

R={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[(xt^{1/3})^{\operatorname{int}},(y_{1}^{5}+y_{1}y_{2}^{6}+y_{3}^{7})t]

be a Rees algebra strictly nested at
H_{1}=V(x)
.
The subvariety
H_{2}=V(x,y_{1})
splits in
X=\operatorname{Spec}(K[x,y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}])
and
D_{y_{1}}
splits on
H_{1}
(so
D_{y_{1}}
vanishes on
{\mathcal{O}}_{H_{2}}=K[y_{1},y_{3}]\subset K[x,y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}]
). Then the generator

(y_{1}^{5}+y_{1}y_{2}^{6}+y_{3}^{7})
can be written in the coefficient form with respect
y_{2}t^{1/5}
:

(y_{1}^{5}+y_{1}y_{2}^{6}+y_{3}^{7})=(y_{1}t^{1/5})^{5}+(y_{1}t^{1/5})y_{2}^{6}t^{4/5}+y_{3}^{7}t],

with the coefficients
c_{y^{1}}=y_{2}^{6}t^{4/5},c_{0}=y_{3}^{7}t
.
Consequently the nested coefficient ideal with respect to
yt^{1/5}
is equal to:

C_{yt^{1/5}}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[(xt^{1/3})^{\operatorname{Int}},(y_{1}^{5}+y_{1}y_{2}^{6}+y_{3}^{7})t]={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[(xt^{1/3},yt^{1/5})^{\operatorname{Int}},y_{2}^{6}t^{4/5},y_{3}^{7}t]

is generated by
(xt^{1/3},yt^{1/5})^{\operatorname{Int}},
and
,y_{2}^{6}t^{4/5}
and
y_{3}^{7}t
.

As an immediate corollary from Lemma
3.5.11
, by considering the restriction
R_{|H_{k}}
we obtain its extension:

Lemma 3.5.24
.

Let
R
be the Rees algebra on
X
nested at
H_{k}
, so that

\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,p}R=\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,p}[(x_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}t^{1/a_{k}})^{\operatorname{Int}},R_{|H_{k}}].

The following conditions are equivalent.

(1)

R\subset{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x_{n}}t^{1/a_{n}}]^{\operatorname{Int}}
.

(2)

{\mathcal{C}}_{\overline{x}_{k+1}t^{1/a_{k+1}}}(R)\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x_{n}}t^{1/a_{n}}]^{\operatorname{Int}}

(3)

{\mathcal{C}}_{\overline{x}_{k+1}t^{1/a_{k+1}}}(R)_{|H_{k+1}}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{H_{k+1}}[\overline{x}_{k+2|H_{k+1}}t^{1/a_{k+2}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{n|H_{k+1}}t^{1/a_{n}}]^{\operatorname{Int}}
.

Proof.

Passing to the completion
\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,p}
we may assume that
R
is strictly nested. Moreover, by replacing
\overline{x}_{k+2}
by
\overline{x}_{k+2|H_{k+1}}
we can assume that
D_{\overline{x}_{k+1}}
is compatible with
{\mathcal{A}}
. Let
R^{\prime}:=R_{|H_{k}}
.
Observe that

{\mathcal{C}}_{\overline{x}_{k+1}t^{1/a_{k+1}}}(R^{\prime})=({\mathcal{C}}_{\overline{x}_{k+1}t^{1/a_{k+1}}}(R))_{|H_{k}}.

By Lemma
3.5.11
,
we have the equivalent conditions for
R^{\prime}
.
On the other hand, since
R
is strictly nested the condition

R\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x_{n}}t^{1/a_{n}}]^{\operatorname{Int}}

is equivalent to

R^{\prime}=R_{|H_{k}}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x}_{k+1|H_{k}}t^{1/a_{k+1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{n|H_{k}}t^{1/a_{n}}]^{\operatorname{Int}}.

Thus the result from Lemma
3.5.11
.
∎

3.6.
Maximal contact

3.6.1.
Cotangent ideal of Rees algebra

From now on until the end of Chapter
3.7
we shall assume that the characteristic of the base field
K
is zero.

First, recall that given an ideal
{\mathcal{I}}
on smooth variety
X
, and a positive integer
a
, the set of points where
{\operatorname{ord}}_{p}({\mathcal{I}})\geq a
is described as
V({\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{\leq a-1}({\mathcal{I}}))
, where
{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{\leq a-1}({\mathcal{I}})
is the ideal generated by all the derivatives
D_{\overline{x}^{\alpha}}(f)
, where
f\in{\mathcal{I}}
and
0\leq|\alpha|\leq a-1
. If the order of
{\mathcal{I}}
is
a
at a point then the order
{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{\leq a-1}({\mathcal{I}})
is equal to
1
and thus it contains a local parameter
u
, called
a maximal contact
, such that
V(u)
contains
V({\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{\leq a-1}({\mathcal{I}}))
. In such a case we shall call the ideal
T^{1/a}({\mathcal{I}}):={\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{\leq a-1}({\mathcal{I}})

cotangent
.
The maximal contact plays a critical role in the classical Hironaka resolution algorithm. It allows for the inductive reduction of the process to the hypersurface of maximal contact.

In our context, the meaning of the maximal contact is different. It is simply the coordinate in the gradation
t^{1/a_{1}}
occurring a some presentation of a maximal
{\mathcal{R}}
-admissible center
{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]
for any Rees algebra
R

Consider the admissibility condition

R\subset{\mathcal{A}}^{\operatorname{Int}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{\operatorname{Int}}.

The idea of the cotangent ideal
T^{1/a_{1}}(R)
is to gather the elements occurring in gradation
t^{1/a_{1}}
, obtained by applying differential operators
{\mathcal{D}}_{\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}}}^{\alpha}
to the generators of
R
, for
\overline{x}
being a complete system of coordinates. The differential operators
{\mathcal{D}}_{\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}}}^{\alpha}
preserve the right side of the admissibility condition (See Lemma
3.4.3
). Then the ideal
T^{1/a_{1}}(R)
is necessarily contained in the gradation
{\mathcal{A}}^{\operatorname{Int}}_{1/a_{1}}
. Conversely by the maximal admissibility condition
T^{1/a_{1}}(R)t^{1/a_{1}}
contains all the coordinates in
\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}}
up to the higher order terms of the gradation
{\mathcal{A}}^{\operatorname{Int}}_{1/a_{1}}
. So, by the Replacement Lemma
3.1.29
, we can assume that the
T^{1/a_{1}}(R)
contains
\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}}
.
This way we will determine
\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}}
- part of the center
{\mathcal{A}}
on the right side of the inclusion with
\overline{x}_{1}\in T^{1/a_{1}}
, which would be called a
maximal contact at a point
. A similar idea was used in the first version of
[
ATW19
]
to interpret the algebra of the center.

Consequently
T^{1/a_{1}}(R)
generalizes
{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{\leq a_{1}-1}({\mathcal{I}})
for the ideal
{\mathcal{I}}
of order
a
at
p
. In particular, if
{\mathcal{I}}t\subset{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{\operatorname{int}}
, then
by Lemma
3.4.3
,

t^{-(a_{1}-1/a_{1})}{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{\leq a_{1}-1}({\mathcal{I}})={\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{\leq a_{1}-1}({\mathcal{I}})t^{1/a_{1}}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{\operatorname{int}}.

Thus
{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{\leq a_{1}-1}({\mathcal{I}})\subset{\mathcal{A}}^{\operatorname{int}}_{1/a_{1}}
.

Definition 3.6.2
.

Given a Rees algebra
R={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[f_{j}t^{b_{j}}]_{j=1,\ldots,s}=\bigoplus R_{a}t^{a}
on an open affine subset
U
of
X
, and a rational number
a_{1}>0
.

\displaystyle T^{1/a_{1}}(R):=\sum_{|\alpha|=b_{j}a_{1}-1}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}{\mathcal{D}}_{\overline{x}^{\alpha}}(f_{j})\quad\subseteq\quad{{\mathcal{T}}}^{\leq 1/a_{1}}(R):=\sum_{\,\,|\alpha|<b_{j}a_{1}}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}{\mathcal{D}}_{\overline{x}^{\alpha}}(f_{j})\subseteq

\displaystyle\subseteq{{\mathcal{T}}}^{<1/a_{1}}(R):=\sum_{\,\,|\alpha|\leq b_{j}a_{1}}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}{\mathcal{D}}_{\overline{x}^{\alpha}}(f_{j}).

Remark 3.6.3
.

As the ideal
T^{1/a_{1}}(R)
is in the gradation
t^{1/a_{1}}
, it can be written in the graded form

T^{1/a_{1}}(R)t^{1/a_{1}}:=\sum_{|\alpha|=b_{j}a_{1}-1}{\mathcal{D}}_{\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}}}^{\alpha}(f_{j}t^{b_{j}}).

Remark 3.6.4
.

The definition intentionally uses a specific representation of Rees algebras for the purpose of streamlining computations. The uniqueness of the center obtained during the resolution process in Section
3.7.1
is automatic and unaffected by any choices made.

The set of generators for
R
can be chosen arbitrarily, and we have the option to select the entire set of homogeneous elements of
R
as generators instead.

In the following examples, when we refer to
D_{X}^{i}({\mathcal{I}})\subset D_{X}^{\leq i}({\mathcal{I}})
, as the ideal generated by all the derivatives
D_{\overline{x}^{\alpha}}(f_{i})
, where
f_{i}\in{\mathcal{I}}
are generators of
{\mathcal{I}}
, and
|\alpha|=i
. This ideal is consistent with the definition of
T^{1/a_{1}}(R)
.

Example 3.6.5
.

Let
a_{1}=3={\operatorname{ord}}_{0}(R)
, where
R={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[(x^{3}+y^{4}+z^{5})t]
.

T^{1/3}(R)t^{1/3}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{2}(x^{3}+y^{4}+z^{5})t^{1/3}]={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[(x,y^{2},z^{3})t^{1/3}]

Example 3.6.6
.

a_{1}=3={\operatorname{ord}}_{0}(R)
, where

R={\mathcal{O}}_{X}([(x^{3}+y^{4}+z^{5})t,(w^{2}x^{4}+z^{7})t^{2},(y^{2}+xz^{2})t^{2/3},w_{1}^{3}t^{3/4}]

Then

\displaystyle T^{1/3}(R)t^{1/3}=(D_{X}^{2}(x^{3}+y^{4}+z^{5}),D_{X}^{5}(w^{2}x^{4}+v^{7}),D_{X}(v^{2}+xz^{2}))t^{1/3}=

\displaystyle=(x,w,v,y^{2},z^{2})t^{1/3},

with local paremeters
x,w,v
in gradation
t^{1/3}
.
The element
w_{1}^{3}t^{3/4}
is of higher order
4>3
. It is thus ignored
as
ba_{1}=3/4\cdot 3=9/4
is not integral. It will not contribute to the set of local parameters of gradation
t^{1/3}
. Corresponding local parameter is
w_{1}t^{1/4}
is of order
4
.

On the other hand the ideal
T^{\leq 1/3}(R)=(x,w,v,y^{2},z^{2},w_{1})
is generated by
T^{1/3}(R)
, and
D^{2}(w_{1}^{3})\sim w_{1}
, for the derivation order
2<ba_{1}=9/4
. The element
w_{1}
is however, in gradation
t^{1/4}
. The ideal
T^{\leq 1/3}(R)
is used only in the context of Lemma
3.6.9
.

In a more general context, we can state the following:

Lemma 3.6.7
.

Let us assume that
{\operatorname{ord}}_{p}(R)=a_{1}
. Then

T^{1/a_{1}}(R)+m_{p}^{2}={\bf C}_{1/a_{1}}+m_{p}^{2}

where
{\bf C}_{1/a_{1}}t^{1/a_{1}}
is the gradation of the algebra

{\bf C}_{\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}}}=\bigoplus{\bf C}_{a}t^{a},

generated by all the elements
D_{\overline{x}t^{1/a_{1}}}^{\alpha}(ft^{a})
, where
f\in R_{a}
, and
|\alpha|<aa_{1}
.
In particular,
T^{1/a_{1}}(R)+m_{p}^{2}
is independent of the choice of generators.
∎

3.6.8.
Singular locus, and the cotangent ideal

The following lemma extends the analogous result for the ideals.

Lemma 3.6.9
.

(1)

{\operatorname{supp}}{\operatorname{ord}}(R,\geq a_{1}):=\{q\in U\mid{\operatorname{ord}}_{q}(R)\geq a_{1}\}=V(\overline{T}^{\leq 1/a_{1}}(R))
.

(2)

{\operatorname{supp}}{\operatorname{ord}}(R,>a_{1}):=\{q\in U\mid{\operatorname{ord}}_{q}(R)>a_{1}\}=V(\overline{T}^{<1/a_{1}}(R)).

Proof.

(1)
{\operatorname{ord}}_{q}(R)\geq a_{1}
if and only if
{\operatorname{ord}}_{q}(f_{j})\geq b_{j}a_{1}
for any
j
iff all
{\mathcal{D}}_{\overline{x}^{\alpha}}(f_{j})
vanish at
q
for
|\alpha|<b_{j}a_{1}
. (2) The reasoning is similar.
∎

3.6.10.
Maximal contact of Rees algebra

The concept of
hypersurfaces of maximal contact
was initially introduced by Hironaka, Abhyankhar, and Giraud, and further developed in the works of Bierstone-Milman, Villamayor, and others. In the case where
{\mathcal{I}}
is an ideal of order
a_{1}
, and no divisors are present a
maximal contact
is defined as a local parameter
u\in T^{1/a}({\mathcal{I}})={\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{a-1}({\mathcal{I}})\subset{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{\leq a-1}({\mathcal{I}})
. Its zero locus
V(u)
includes the set
V({\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{\leq a-1}({\mathcal{I}}))
, which consists of the
equimultiple locus
of all points where
{\operatorname{ord}}_{p}({\mathcal{I}})=a
.

Our definition of maximal contact is intended for Rees algebras. From our perspective, a maximal contact refers to a partial coordinate system
\overline{x}_{1}
found in
t^{1/a_{1}}
-gradation of a maximal
R
-admissible center

{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}[\overline{x}_{1}t^{1/a_{1}},\ldots,\overline{x}_{k}t^{1/a_{k}}]^{\operatorname{int}}
